CAD: Debiasing the Lasso with inaccurate covariate model
Michael Celentano and Andrea Montanari

Abstract
We consider the problem of estimating a low-dimensional parameter in high-dimensional linear regression. Constructing an approximately unbiased estimate of the parameter of interest is a crucial step towards performing statistical inference. Several authors suggest to orthogonalize both the variable of interest and the outcome with respect to the nuisance variables, and then regress the residual outcome with respect to the residual variable. This is possible if the covariance structure of the regressors is perfectly known, or is sufficiently structured that it can be estimated accurately from data (e.g., the precision matrix is sufficiently sparse). Here we consider a regime in which the covariate model can only be estimated inaccurately, and hence existing debiasing approaches are not guaranteed to work. When errors in estimating the covariate model are correlated with errors in estimating the linear model parameter, an incomplete elimination of the bias occurs. We propose the Correlation Adjusted Debiased Lasso (CAD) , which nearly eliminates this bias in some cases, including cases in which the estimation errors are neither negligible nor orthogonal. We consider a setting in which some unlabeled samples might be available to the statistician alongside labeled ones (semi-supervised learning), and our guarantees hold under the assumption of jointly Gaussian covariates. The new debiased estimator is guaranteed to cancel the bias in two cases: ( 1 ) (1) when the total number of samples (labeled and unlabeled) is larger than the number of parameters, or ( 2 ) (2) when the covariance of the nuisance (but not the effect of the nuisance on the variable of interest) is known. Neither of these cases is treated by state-of-the-art methods.



1
Introduction

An important task in high-dimensional and semi-parametric statistics is to estimate and perform inference on a low-dimensional parameter in the presence of a high-dimensional nuisance.
We study this problem in the context of a random-design linear model

\begin{gathered}{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{w}\beta+{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}+\sigma\boldsymbol{z},\end{gathered}

(1)

where
{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
has rows
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{N}(0,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
,

\boldsymbol{w}={\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}+\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp}
,
and
\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp},\boldsymbol{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{n})
independent of
{\boldsymbol{X}}
.
The target of estimation and inference is
\beta
.
We consider a setting in which
n
is comparable to and possibly smaller than
p
,
but a larger unlabelled data set might be available for estimating
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

Our ability to estimate and perform inference on
\beta
is substantially improved by knowledge of the distribution of the features
(\boldsymbol{w},{\boldsymbol{X}})
, parameterized by
\kappa,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
Consider, for example, the debiased Lasso.
The debiased Lasso is a one-step correction to the Lasso estimate defined by

\begin{gathered}(\widehat{\beta},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}):=\argmin_{(b,\boldsymbol{t})}\Big\{\frac{1}{2n}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{w}b-{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{t}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{n}}(|b|+\|\boldsymbol{t}\|_{1})\Big\},\\
\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}:=\widehat{\beta}+\frac{1}{n\hat{\tau}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}-\boldsymbol{w}\widehat{\beta}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\,,\end{gathered}

(2)

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is an estimate of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\hat{\tau}^{2}
is a normalization coefficient.
Both of these are estimated from data or on the basis of some knowledge of the data distributiomn.
A substantial line of research develops debiasing procedure which differ in how
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\hat{\tau}
are defined, see

[
ZZ14
,
vdGBRD14
,
JM14b
,
JM13
,
JM14a
,
JM18
,
BZ20a
]

for a few entrypoints in this literature.

Under appropriate conditions and up to constant and logarithmic factors,
the Lasso achieves estimation error in
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
comparable to what is achievable when the support of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is known
[
BRT09
,
BvdG11
]
.
These guarantees apply only to estimation of the high-dimensional parameter
(\beta,{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
as a whole.
For any given coordinate (in particular, for the parameter of interest
\beta
) the Lasso error may be substantially
larger than that achievable were the support of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
known.
1
1

1

For example, an explicit calculation with design-matrix given in Example 7.1 of
[
BvdG11
]
shows that the error in a single coordinate can be on the order
\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{s_{\theta}\log(p/s_{\theta})/n})
if we choose
\lambda=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{\log(p/s_{\theta})})
in Eq. (
2
).

The debiased Lasso (
2
) targets estimation of a single coordinate and can improve error guarantees for it.
Moreover, in certain regimes
\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}
is approximately normal,
permitting the construction of
p
-values and confidence intervals.

As we have already alluded to,
the debiased Lasso benefits substantially from knowledge of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
When
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is unknown,
the best bounds establish
\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta=O_{p}\big((\sigma/\kappa)\big(1/\sqrt{n}+(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})\log(p)/n\big)\big)

[
JM18
,
BZ20a
]
, where
s_{\theta}:=\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{0}
and
s_{\gamma}:=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{0}

are the numbers of nonzero coefficients (these bounds can be improved to cover the case of approximately sparse vectors).
The
1/\sqrt{n}
term is a parametric rate and would be present even if
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
were known.
The term
(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})\log(p)/n
captures the cost of not knowing the nuisance parameters
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
It dominates the estimation error unless
s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma}=O(\sqrt{n}/\log(p))
,
a much stronger requirement than that required for consistency of the Lasso for either
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
or
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
In contrast, if
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is known,
then by setting
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and appropriately choosing
\hat{\tau},\lambda
,
the debiased Lasso achieves the parametric rate even if
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is fully dense and
s_{\theta}\log p/n=\Theta(1)

[
JM14b
,
JM13
,
MM21
,
BZ20b
,
CMW20
]
.
A related procedure achieves paramteric rates provided the bound
\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{0}\leq s_{\theta}
is replaced by the bound
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=O(\sigma^{2})

[
ZB18b
,
ZB18a
,
BFZ18
]
.

A key motivation of our work is that approaches to dealing with unknown
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

[
ZZ14
,
vdGBRD14
,
JM14a
,
JM18
,
BZ20a
]

operate essentially by reduction to the case of known
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Roughly speaking, these papers assume either that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is sparse enough that it can be accurately estimated from the data,
or that
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is sparse enough that the uncertainty on
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
has negligible effect.
Under the condition
s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma}=O(\sqrt{n}/\log(p))
, the uncertainty on
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is negligible and
does not need to be accounted for in the resulting inference procedures.

The current paper considers a regime in which
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
cannot be estimated accurately, and —for this reason—
standard debiasing procedures are unsuccessful.
We show that —in some cases— these procedures can be corrected to cancel the residual bias,
and propose the
Correlation Adjusted Debiased Lasso (CAD)
to implement the correction.
Under the assumption of
(correlated) Gaussian covariates, we prove that CAD yields nearly unbiased estimates.
An illustration is provided Figure
1
(see Sections
2
and
5
for further explanation).
Although the Lasso is the primary motivation for the current work, our theory will also establish the success of correlation adjusted debiasing for ridge regression.

We work in a semisupervised model in which the statistician has access to
N
samples,
some of which are labeled and some of which are unlabeled.
A subset
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\subset[N]
of units, all of which are labelled, will be used for the estimation of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, which we call the

outcome regression
.
A subset
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}\subset[N]
of units, which can contain both labelled and unlabelled units,
will be used for the estimation of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, which we call the
precision regression

(see Section
2
for a complete description).
In other words
\{(y_{i},w_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}
are used for the outcome regression
and
\{(w_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}
are used for precision regression. The two are then combined
to construct the CAD estimate of
\beta
.
We assume
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}=[N]
(so that all units are used in either the outcome or precision regression),
and we allow for
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
to be overlapping.
In particular, we may use both labeled and unlabeled data to estimate
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

Part of earlier work assumes no unlabeled samples and hence
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is estimated from the same labeled samples as
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
which is a special case of our setting.
Other earlier results assume
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
to be perfectly known.
The latter can be justified by assuming access to a very large number of unlabeled data (see, e.g.,
[
JM18
]
).
By considering a semisupervised model, our analysis covers these two extremes as special cases,
and allows us to quantify how many additional samples are needed to
estimate
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
accurately enough.

We prove that CAD is nearly unbiased in at least two cases:

(1)
When sufficientlly many samples are available for the precision regression to be underparametrized, namely

n_{\gamma}>(1+\varepsilon)p
, with
\varepsilon
bounded away from
0
;

(2)
When the covariance structure of the nuisance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is known.
We emphasize that none of these cases can be treated using existing methodology.

1.1
Related literature

Our results cover the proportional asymptotics in which
n,p
are both large with
n\asymp p
. This is the most challenging regime,
since the effect of the uncertainty on
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is comparable to or larger than the intrinsic parametric error
(\sigma/\kappa)/\sqrt{n}
.
However, we emphasize that:
(i)
Our analysis is fully non-asymptotic and we establish concentration bounds
on the newly developed estimator that are valid at finite
p,n
;
(ii)
These concentration bounds do not degrade as
n/p\to\infty
,
and hence cover a broad range of high-dimensional regimes.

Over the last few years, several mathematical techniques have been developed to characterize high-dimensional statistical problems
in the proportional regime
[
BM11
,
ALMT14
,
DM16
,
EK18
,
RP16
,
BKM+19
]
.
Gordon’s Gaussian comparison inequality
[
Gor85
,
Gor88
]
is particularly useful in the study of regularized
convex M-estimators.
As first pointed out in
[
Sto13
,
TOH15a
]
,
such estimators can be written as saddle points of convex-concave cost functions, and Gordon’s inequality can be used to
sharply characterize their asymptotic properties, see e.g.

[
TOH15a
,
TAH18
,
DKT19
,
MRSY20
,
LS20
]
.

The most closely related pieces of work are
[
MM21
]
, which provides a non-asymptotic characterization of the Lasso
in the proportional regime, for uncorrelated Gaussian designs, and
[
CMW20
]
which generalizes it to correlated designs.
The paper
[
CMW20
]
also develops a procedure for statistical inference on low-dimensional parameters
under the assumption that effect of the nuisance variables (encoded by
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
) is known.

Our focus is on the case in which
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
needs to be
estimated from data. This leads to a simultaneous regression problem, and a direct generalization of the
Gaussian comparison approach of
[
Gor85
,
Gor88
,
Sto13
,
TOH15a
]

simply fails. Namely, the ‘correct’ dominating Gaussian process (the one that yields the right characterization) does not satisfy the
conditions of Gordon’s inequality.

We overcome this problem by introducing a new proof techniques, based on a two-step argument.
Given two simultaneous regression problems, we analyze the first one using the Gaussian comparison approach.
We then show that we can condition on the result of the first regression and develop a ‘conditional Gordon inequality’
to characterize the conditional behavior of the second. Putting everything together, we obtain a joint characterization of the
two regressions. The only earlier example of a joint characterization of two estimators in the proportional asymptotics is the one of
[
MTV20
]
,
which is limited however to the special case of linear regression and spectral estimation.

In recent years it has become popular to base
inference in possibly non-linear regression models on complete knowledge of the distribution of covariates.
This approach is often referred to as
model-X inference.

Prominent examples include knockoffs and conditional randomization testing
[
CFJL18
,
KR20
,
LKJR20
]
.
Although model-X assumptions are sometimes based on scientific domain knowledge (see, e.g.,
[
BSSC20
,
SSC18
]
),
they are often also justifed by informal appeals to estimates coming from large unlabelled data sets
[
KR20
]
.
In the context of linear models, our work provides rigorous justification for these claims, and quantifies the amount of
additional data needed to estimate the covariates model.

In particular, we clarify that the success of the procedure relies on more than just the size of the error in the model for the features;
it also relies on how these models were estimated and how these estimates were incorporated into our analysis of the labeled data.
Although random-design linear models are outside of the model-X paradigm—we posit both a model for the covariates and the outcome variable—we have no reason to expect model misspecification in model-X procedures to be any more benign.
In fact, recent work has shown close relationships between debiasing estimators and certain conditional randomization tests
[
CMW20
]
.

The scope of debiasing procedures was generalized in
[
CCD+18
]
and follow-up work by considering a broader set of
(not necessarily linear) models for the nuisance. As in the original debiasing literature

[
ZZ14
,
vdGBRD14
,
JM14a
]
, these authors assume conditions under which the error in estimating the nuisance model can be safely neglected.
Further, they deal with nonlinear nuisance models by using sample splitting or crossfitting, which avoids mathematical difficulties but may result in inefficiencies in a proportional regime.

As discussed below, one of the elements of the CAD estimator

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is given by the so-called
degrees of freedom adjustement
, which was introduced in
[
JM14b
]
and
analyzed for general random designs in
[
BZ20a
,
BZ20b
]
. As further discussed in the next section, the
degrees of freedom adjustement is not sufficient in the case of unknown precision model. In Section
2.2
we further compare our results
with the ones of
[
BZ20a
,
BZ20b
]
.

2
Correlation adjusted debiasing

2.1
Description of correlation adjusted debiasing

Figure 1:
Comparing different debiasing methods, for different data distributions (see Section
5
for definitions).
Left: block model with
\ell_{\textrm{b}}=10
,
q=1
,
\mu=2
,
\sigma=1
.
Right: circulant model with
\mu=2
,
\sigma=1
and inverse covariance specified in Eq. (
28
).
In both cases,
n_{\theta}=200
,
n_{\gamma}=600
,
p=400
.
Bands correspond to
20\%
and
80\%
percentiles of various debiased estimates over
500
repetitions.

In order to motivate the correlation adjusted debiasing (CAD) procedure,
it is worth recalling some important steps in standard analyses of the debiased Lasso.
As mentioned above, various debiasing procedures differ in the way the estimate of the nuisance parameters
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

and normalization coefficients
\hat{\tau}
appearing in Eq. (
2
) are defined.
For our discussion, we assume
\hat{\tau}^{2}=\langle\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle/n
.
This choice captures many of the important intuitions, and avoids some inessential complications.
With this definition, some straightforward algebra gives

\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta=\frac{\langle\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\rangle}{n\hat{\tau}^{2}}-\beta=\frac{\langle\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\sigma\boldsymbol{z}-{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\rangle}{n\hat{\tau}^{2}}.

(3)

Writing the debiased Lasso in this form reveals that the debiased estimate is a rescaled correlation of the residuals of two regressions:
the feature of interest
\boldsymbol{w}
on the remaining covariates
{\boldsymbol{X}}
and the outcome
{\boldsymbol{y}}
on the remaining covariates.
This is not surprising.
In the population,
\beta
is identified in precisely this way.
Indeed,
we may write the linear model (
1
)
in the form

{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{X}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}+\sigma\boldsymbol{z}+\kappa\beta\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp},

(4)

where
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:={\boldsymbol{\theta}}+\beta{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
The parameter
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
results from regressing
{\boldsymbol{y}}
onto
{\boldsymbol{X}}
in the population.
Then,

\beta=\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}})]}{n\kappa^{2}}.

(5)

Equation (
3
) reveals that the debiased Lasso—at least in the case that
\hat{\tau}^{2}=\langle\boldsymbol{w}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{w}\rangle/n
—is an empirical form of this identifying equation.

If
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}={\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
then it is easy to see that
\hat{\tau}^{2}=\kappa^{2}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
,
whence
\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta)=\langle\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp},\sigma\boldsymbol{z}\rangle/(\sqrt{n}\hat{\tau}^{2})\approx\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma^{2}/\kappa^{2})
.
This is exactly what we would get in the low dimensional setting in which we regress
\beta\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp}+\sigma\boldsymbol{z}
on
\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp}
.
In the high-dimensional setting,
performance is degraded relative to this benchmark due to uncertainty in
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
In fact,
we may decompose

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta)=\frac{\langle\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp},\sigma\boldsymbol{z}\rangle}{\sqrt{n}\hat{\tau}^{2}}-\frac{\langle\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp},{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\rangle}{\sqrt{n}\hat{\tau}^{2}}-\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),\sigma\boldsymbol{z}\rangle}{\sqrt{n}\hat{\tau}^{2}}+\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}),{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\rangle}{\sqrt{n}\hat{\tau}^{2}}.

(6)

The final three terms are errors due to uncertainty in
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
and are the source of the non-parametric rate
(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})\log p/n
.

Rather than construct an estimate of
\beta
as a one-step correction to the Lasso estimate
(\widehat{\beta},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
,
we use the identifying equation (
5
) as our starting point.
Recall that the statistician uses the samples indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\subset[N]
(all labeled) to estimate
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
and the ones indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}\subset[N]
(which can be either labeled or unlabeled) to estimate
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
These two sets have sizes
n_{\theta}:=|\mathcal{I}_{\theta}|
,
n_{\gamma}:=|\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}|
and may overlap or even coincide..

A natural approach to estimating
\beta
is to use an empirical version of the identifying equation Eq. (
5
) with plug-in estimates for
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
:

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}=\frac{1}{\hat{\kappa}^{2}}\frac{1}{n_{\theta}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}(y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})(w_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}).

(7)

Here
\hat{\kappa}
is a consistent estimator of
\kappa
(we will leave this unspecified for the moment),
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are estimates of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
coming from samples
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
, respectively.
Although our discussion up to this point has focused on the Lasso estimator,
which has received the most focus in the debiasing literature,
we will also develop theory for ridge regression.
In particular, we consider using estimators

\begin{gathered}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2n_{\theta}}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\Omega_{\theta}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}:=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2n_{\gamma}}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\Omega_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\end{gathered}

(8)

where the penalty is, for
\mathsf{x}\in\{\theta,\gamma\}
,

\Omega_{\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\pi}):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{\mathsf{x}}}}\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{x}}}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}\quad&\text{for ridge regression},\\
\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{x}}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{x}}}}\|\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{1}\quad&\text{for Lasso},\end{cases}

(9)

and
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\mathcal{I}|}
is the vector of outcomes corresponding to those units in a set
\mathcal{I}
,
and
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\mathcal{I}|\times p}
is a matrix with rows corresponding to those units in
\mathcal{I}
.
When
n_{\gamma}>p
,
we may set
\lambda_{\gamma}=0
, which gives the least-squares estimator for
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

The estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
can be biased due to errors in estimating
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
For instance, when both
n_{\theta},n_{\gamma}>p
, and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are estimated using least squares,
elementary linear regression theory implies that
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
has a bias that is of order

p/n_{\theta}
. In the case of the Lasso, the bias is of order
\tilde{O}\big((s_{\theta}/n_{\theta})\vee(s_{\gamma}/n_{\gamma})\big)

(neglecting logarithmic factors).
Figure
1
illustrates this phenomenon on two synthetic data distributions (see Section
5
for definitions
and more extensive simulations).
In this case
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
: we use all the available samples for the precision regression, and all the labeled samples
for the outcome regression. Further,
n_{\theta}<p
while
n_{\gamma}>p
, and we use the Lasso for the outcome regression and least
squares for the precision regression. We notice that both the Lasso estimate

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{Lasso}}
and the naive debiased Lasso
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
are strongly biased. Indeed the bias is
of the same order as the true coefficient size.
In Section
3
, we dicuss the sources of bias for the naive estimator, and its relation to the correlation in the errors in estimating
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

It convenient to focus on the proportional regime in which
n_{\theta},n_{\gamma}\asymp p
, and (in the case of sparse vectors)

s_{\theta},s_{\gamma}\asymp p
as well. In this case, the naive debiased estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
has a
bias that does not vanish asymptotically.

The CAD estimator is a correction to the naïve estimator which removes the major sources of bias of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
.
The correction to the naïve estimator involves the
degrees of freedom
of each regression method,
defined for
\mathsf{x}\in\{\theta,\gamma\}

\operatorname{Tr}\Big({\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}})}{\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}}\Big).

(10)

For the regression methods above, this takes the explicit form

\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\mathsf{x}}:=\begin{cases}p\quad&\text{for least squares,}\\
\operatorname{Tr}\big(\big(\frac{1}{n_{\mathsf{x}}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{\mathsf{x}}}}\,\lambda_{\mathsf{x}}\mathbf{I}_{p}\big)^{-1}\frac{1}{n_{\mathsf{x}}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{x}}}\big)\quad&\text{for ridge regression,}\\
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{0}\quad&\text{for Lasso.}\end{cases}

(11)

The naïve estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
involves the empirical correlation between unadjusted regression residuals.
It is intuitively clear that these residuals are smaller than in the population when
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta}
(for the outcome regression) or

i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
(for the precision regression).
This effect can be compensated by defining the following degrees of freedom adjusted residuals.

r^{\theta}_{i}=\begin{cases}y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\quad&\text{if }i\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta},\\
\frac{y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}{1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\theta}/n_{\theta}}\quad&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta},\end{cases}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;r^{\gamma}_{i}=\begin{cases}w_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\quad&\text{if }i\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma},\\
\frac{y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}{1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma}/n_{\gamma}}\quad&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}.\end{cases}

(12)

Define

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\hat{\kappa}^{2}}\frac{1}{n_{\theta}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}r_{i}^{\theta}r_{i}^{\gamma}.

(13)

The suggestion of replacing
y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
by
r_{i}^{\theta}
has already appeared in the literature

[
JM14b
,
BZ20a
,
CMW20
]
, under the name of ‘degrees of freedom adjustement’.
However, earlier work assumes
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
known, and adjusted only the outcome residuals.
If only the outcome residuals were adjusted in the definition of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
,
we would arrive at certain earlier forms of the debiased Lasso with degrees-of-freedom adjustment, as indicated by Eq. (
3
).
As anticipated, we instead treat
the uncertainty in the outcome and precision model on the same footing, applying the degrees of freedom adjustment to the residuals from both.

The estimate
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
corrects some—but not all—sources of bias in
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
.
This is confirmed by Figure
1
:
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
has a much smaller bias than

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
or the Lasso estimate
\widehat{\beta}
in both data generating models.
However, the bias is non-vanishing in both cases.
The debiased estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
, which is the main focus on this paper, will correct the sources of bias in
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
as well.

In order to correct for the bias of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
, it is useful to introduce the debiased outcome model

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}+\frac{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})}{n_{\theta}-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\theta}},

(14)

and similarly define
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}
. Note that, if
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is the least squares estimator, then

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(and similarly for
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
).
Finally, we can provide the correlation adjusted debiased (CAD) estimator, which gives a nearly-unbiased estimate of
\beta
:

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}=\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}+\frac{n_{\theta\gamma}/p}{(n_{\theta}/p)(n_{\gamma}/p)}\Big(1-\frac{\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma}}{p}-\frac{\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\theta}}{p}\Big)\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}-\frac{\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}}{\hat{\kappa}^{2}},

(15)

where
n_{\theta\gamma}=|\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}|
,
and we use the noise estimate (which we will see is consistent)

\hat{\kappa}^{2}:=\Big(1+\frac{p}{n_{\gamma}}-2\frac{\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma}}{n_{\gamma}}\Big)\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{\gamma}(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma}/n_{\gamma})^{2}}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}.

(16)

The second and third terms in Eq. (
15
) do not appear in earlier work and are entirely due to the inaccurate estimation of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
:
indeed, they vanish if we let
n_{\gamma}\to\infty
, in which case
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}\to{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Importantly, these terms do not only depend on the size of the error in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
but also on its correlation with the error in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
This depends in a non-trivial way on the estimation method used and the extent to which
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
overlap.
Because these terms correct for the bias induced by the correlated errors in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we refer to them as
correlation adjustments
. (See Section
3
for more discussion on this point).

Although any consistent estimate
\hat{\kappa}
of
\kappa
can be used in Eqs. (
13
) and (
14
) without impacting the consistency of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
,
we will use the estimate
\hat{\kappa}
provided in Eq. (
16
) throughout our analysis.
It is a generalization of the noise estimate provided in
[
BEM13
]
(see discussion in Section
6
).
In the case that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is the least-squares estimator,
this estimate takes a simple form.
One can then check that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Recalling that in this case
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma}=p
,
we see that
\hat{\kappa}^{2}=\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{2}^{2}/(n_{\gamma}(1-p/n_{\gamma})^{2})
, which is the standard noise variance estimate for least-squares.
In this case, computing
\hat{\kappa}^{2}
does not require knowledge of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

Note also that the first two terms in Eq. (
15
) and the first term in Eq. (
16
) can be evaluated from data, while for the third term in Eq. (
15
) and second term in Eq. (
16
) this is not always the case,
since they require knowledge of the correlation of the nuisance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
Nevertheless, there are at least two important
cases in which
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is a practical statistical estimate:
(1)
When least squares is used for the precision model in which case
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
;
(2)
When
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}

is known or can be estimated, in which case the third term in Eq. (
15
) and second term in Eq. (
16
) can be estimated as well.
While the second scenario might appear to
bring us back to a similar assumption as the one we want to avoid (knowledge of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
), it is not quite the same,
as discussed below.
Also, we expect that an inaccurate estimate of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
in Eq. (
15
) will have a smaller effect than an inaccurate estimate of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, but leave this point to future investigation.

2.2
Main result and discussion

We postpone a complete statement of the assumptions in our main result to Section
4
.
Our main result is the following.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under assumptions
A1
and
A2
,
there exist constants
c_{0},C_{0}>0
depending uniquely on the constants in those assumptions (and, in particular, independent of

\beta,{\boldsymbol{\theta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
), such that

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(|\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta|>\sqrt{\frac{p^{1-c_{0}}}{n_{\theta}\wedge n_{\gamma}}}\Big)\leq\frac{C_{0}}{p^{2}}\,.\end{gathered}

(17)

Remark 2.2
.

This theorem implies in particular that
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is a consistent estimate of
\beta
in the proportional asymptotics,
despite
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
being unknown.
Namely, for any sequence of problems with
p\to\infty
, and both
n_{\theta},s_{\theta}\geq\delta_{0}p
and
n_{\gamma}\geq\delta_{0}p
(for some constant
\delta_{0}>0
), we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{\longrightarrow}}0\,.

(18)

This occurs even when
s_{\theta}=\Omega(n_{\theta})
and
s_{\gamma}=\Omega(n_{\gamma})
as well.
As discussed above, the same is not achieved by earlier debiasing procedures.

Remark 2.3
.

We do not expect the upper bound on the size of
|\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta|
in Theorem
2.1

to be optimal: namely we expect
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta
to be significantly smaller than
O(\sqrt{p^{1-c_{0}}/n})
.
As a consequence, we do not propose to use Theorem
2.1
as a basis to construct confidence intervals, which would be overly conservative.

Nevertheless, in Section
5
we provide simulation evidence that the CAD estimator is approximately normal and centered on the true parameter. This suggests that developing inference procedures based on
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}

is a promising avenue for future research.

Remark 2.4
.

It is instructive to compare Theorem
2.1
with earlier results for the Lasso or debiased Lasso.
Tight coordinatewise bounds for the Lasso estimator
\widehat{\beta}
were proved in
[
Lou08
]
. These imply

|\widehat{\beta}-\beta|=O(\sqrt{\log p/n})
but assume an incoherence condition that can only hold in the very sparse regime
s_{\theta}\ll\sqrt{n}
.
The best bounds for the debiased Lasso (with degrees of freedom adjustement) yield
[
BZ20a
]
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta|\lesssim\frac{(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})\log p}{n}\wedge\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

(19)

Considering for instance the case in which
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
has
s_{\gamma}
non-zero entries of magnitude
1/\sqrt{s_{\gamma}}
, the second term dominates.
The bound then reduces to
|\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{d}}-\beta|\lesssim\sqrt{s_{\gamma}\frac{\log p}{n}}
which is larger than one if
s_{\gamma}\asymp p\asymp n
(even if
n>p
).
In contrast, our approach yields
|\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta|=o_{p}(1)
with an estimator that can be computed from data as soon as
n_{\gamma}>p
.

We finally compare our upper bound with lower bounds available in the literature, for the case of unknown precision matrix.
As proven in
[
CG17
,
JM18
]
,
in the fully supervised setting
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{I}_{\theta}
(
n_{\theta}=n_{\gamma}=:n
), and if

s_{\theta}\lesssim p^{\eta}\wedge(n/\log p)
for some
\eta<1/2
,
no estimator of
\beta
achieves worst-case error
o_{p}\big((\sigma/\kappa)\big(1/\sqrt{n}+(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})\log p/n\big)\big)
over the parameter class (see
[
JM18
, Proposition 4.2]
)

\Big\{(\kappa,\sigma,\beta,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\Bigm|\kappa\geq c_{1},\,\sigma\leq c_{2},\,\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{2}\leq c_{3},\,\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{0}\leq s_{\gamma},\,\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{0}\leq s_{\theta},\,c_{3}\mathbf{I}\preceq{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\preceq c_{4}\mathbf{I}\Big\}.

(20)

Theorem
2.1
guarantees that
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
achieves an error that is significantly smaller than this
lower bound, provided

(s_{\theta}\wedge s_{\gamma})^{2}\gg np^{1-c^{\prime}_{0}}
, which is for instance the case in the proportional asymptotics.

One might think that the improvement of our estimator relative to this minimax lower bound results from our use of

n_{\gamma}>p
unlabeled samples for the precision model (if we use least squares for the latter), or from our assumption that

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is known (if Lasso or ridge are used for the precision model).
However, this is not the case.
Indeed, inspecting the proofs of
[
CG17
]
and
[
JM18
, Proposition 4.2]
reveals that the minimax lower bound
applies also to the parameter space with restriction
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbf{I}
.

The reason why our upper bound does not contradict the lower bounds of
[
CG17
,
JM18
]
is
that the latter only apply to
s_{\theta}=o(p^{1/2})
.
As far as we know, proving sharp lower bounds for
s_{\theta}\gtrsim p^{1/2}
remains an open problem.

3
Why are the naïve estimators biased?

To build intuition about reason the naïve estimators
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}

are biased, it is useful to consider a simpler estimator in which the correlation between residuals is computed on different units
from the ones used to estimate
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
Namely, consider
\mathcal{I}_{\theta},\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}\subset[N]
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq[N]\setminus(\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\gamma})
(where, unlike above,
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
is a strict subset of
[N]
).
Define

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{split}}:=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})(w_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}).

(21)

For simplicity (and because we are only interested here in building intuition), we set
\hat{\kappa}^{2}=\kappa^{2}
.
We compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{split}}\bigm|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(\sigma z_{i}+\kappa\beta w_{i}^{\perp})\kappa w_{i}^{\perp}\bigm|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}]+\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}})\bigm|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}]

(22)

\displaystyle=\beta+\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}/\kappa^{2}.

The sample splitting estimator is biased when the error in estimating
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is aligned (in the sense of having a non-vanishing inner product)
with the error in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
There are two reasons these errors might be aligned.
The first reason is that the estimators for
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are themselves biased—as is the case when using the Lasso or ridge regression—and these biases are non-orthogonal.
For example,
if the signs of
\bar{\theta}_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
are the same and both are estimated using the Lasso,
we expect the errors
\hat{\theta}_{i}-\bar{\theta}_{i}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{i}-\gamma_{i}
to also be aligned,
even if
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are computed on disjoint samples of data (i.e.,
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}=\emptyset
).
We call this source of bias
correlated shrinkage bias
.
The second reason the error in estimating
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
may be aligned with the error in estimating
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is that the estimates may come from the same or overlapping samples.
Indeed,
in the case
n_{\gamma}=n_{\theta}>p
and both
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are computed with least-squares on the same units,
we do not expect the errors
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
to be uncorrelated.
We call this source of bias
overlapping samples bias
.

Characterizing the correlation
\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
theoretically
is a key technical contribution of the present work.
As mentioned above, this requires the joint characterization of two regularized regressions,
a type of problem that goes beyond the scope of previous work
[
Kar13
,
DW18
,
BM11
,
TOH15b
,
MM21
,
CMW20
,
MTV20
]
.
More important than characterizing the correlated shrinkage bias and overlapping samples bias theoretically is correcting them empirically.
One proposal would be to use sample splitting again, computing
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
with different samples.
That is,
we could take
\mathcal{I}_{\theta},\mathcal{I}_{\gamma},\mathcal{I}
be mutually disjoint.
This approach would eliminate overlapping samples bias but not correlated shrinkage bias.
Thus, sample splitting does not correct for bias in the estimation of
\beta
.
Moreover, it comes at the cost of statistical efficiency.

One attempt to improve the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{split}}
is to instead use a leave-one-out estimator:

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{loo}}:=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\frac{1}{n_{\theta}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\theta}}(y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(-i)})(w_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{(-i)}),

(23)

where for each
i
the estimate
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(-i)}
is computed using all labeled units except unit
i
(and similarly for
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, except that unlabeled units may also be used).
For a single term in this average, we have
\mathbb{E}[(y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(-i)})(w_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{(-i)})/\kappa^{2}\bigm|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{-i},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{-i}]=\beta+\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(-i)}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{(-i)}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}/\kappa^{2}
,
where
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{-i},{\boldsymbol{X}}_{-i}
contain the data from all units except unit
i
.
Thus, the leave-one-out estimator is also subject to correlated shrinkage bias and overlapping samples bias.
The primary motivation to use it in place of the sample-splitting estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{split}}
is to improve statistical efficiency.
Indeed, the leave-one-out estimator uses more samples to estimate the nuisance parameters
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, and it averages over more samples to compute the estimate
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{loo}}
.

One drawback of the leave-one-out procedure is the computational cost of computing it:
in principle, it requires computing
2n_{\theta}
regression estimates, which may be prohibitive if
n_{\theta}
and
p
are large.
The estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
, introduced in Eq. (
13
),
attempts to approximate the leave-one-out estimator while only requiring that we compute two high-dimensional regression estimates.
It is based on the following remarkable approximation which holds for the in-sample residuals (that is, those
i
which are used to compute
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
):

y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\approx(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\theta}/n_{\theta})(y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(-i)}).

(24)

The analogous approximation holds for the precision regression residuals.
We do not quantify the quality of this approximation in the present work,
though similar approximations have appeared elsewhere
[
Kar13
,
BZ20a
,
YYMD21
]
.
Based on this approximation, the degrees-of-freedom adjustment in Eq. (
12
)
is an attempt to approximate the leave-one-out residuals without computing them directly.
Without being rigorous, we expect
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}\approx\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{loo}}
, where
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
is much easier to compute.
As we have already mentioned,
if we were to apply the degrees-of-freedom adjustment only to the outcome residuals in the definition of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
,
we would arrive at certain earlier forms of the debiased Lasso with degrees-of-freedom adjustment.
Such methods can be thought of as attempts to approximate a leave-one-out estimator that uses oracle knowledge of (or a very good estimate of)
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.

The discussion in the previous paragraph motivates why we use the degrees-of-freedom adjusted estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
in place of the unadjusted estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
defined in Eq. (
7
).
In particular,
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
corrects a bias present in the unadjusted estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
, which
we call
overfitting bias
.
It results from the fact that in-sample residuals—i.e., the residual
y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
when
(y_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})
is used in the fitting of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
—tend to be smaller than the residual
y_{i}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
when
(y_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})
is not used in the fitting of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
This phenomenon will be unsurprising to many statisticians, and is consistent with Eq. (
24
).
Although
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
corrects the overfitting bias present in the unadjusted estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
,
it is still subject to correlated shrinkage bias and overlapping samples bias.
Indeed, it approximates the leave-one-out estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{loo}}
, which is subject to these biases as well.

The primary innovation of the correlation adjusted debiasing estimate —and the reason for its name— is to correct for the bias in
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\prime}
due to the correlation
\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
Importantly, CAD is a fully empirical procedure that does not require any prior knowledge of either
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
or
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
In the case that least-squares is used for the precision regression (provided
n_{\gamma}>p
),
it also does not require any prior knowledge of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
We emphasize that even when least-squares is used for the precision regression,
the overlapping samples bias need not be 0,
so that correcting for it in this case is still non-trivial.
Because we do not resort to sample splitting, we correct the bias without sacrificing statistical efficiency.
Although the discussion in this section has been non-rigorous, Theorem
2.1
shows that the degrees-of-freedom and correlation adjustments to the naive estimate
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
together achieve the desired goal of
removing all non-vanishing bias.

We summarize the adjustments which enter the construction of the CAD estimator and the biases they correct for:

1.

Degrees-of-freedom adjustment.

The degrees-of-freedom adjustment scales in-sample residuals by
(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\mathsf{x}}/n_{\mathsf{x}})^{-1}
(see Eq. (
12
)).
Its purpose is to correct for overfitting bias.
Overfitting bias occurs because in-sample residuals behave differently than out-of-sample residuals; in particular, they are shrunk towards zero.
There is a nearly deterministic relationship between in- and out-of-sample residuals,
with the former being
(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\mathsf{x}}/n_{\mathsf{x}})
times the latter.
The degrees-of-freedom adjustment applied to the outcome model has appeared in previous work
[
JM14b
,
BZ20a
,
BZ20b
]
.

2.

Correlation adjustment.

The correlation adjustment refers to the second and third terms in Eq. (
15
),
and it corrects for bias due to the correlation of the estimation errors
\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
This correlation results from correlated shrinkage bias and overlapping samples bias.
Correcting for it is the primary innovation of the current work.

Correlated shrinkage bias occurs when the bias of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are both non-zero and aligned with each other.
Because least-squares regression is unbiased,
correlated shrinkage bias only occurs when both
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are fit using a penalized procedure.
Note that when either
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
or
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is fit with least-squares, the third term in Eq. (
15
) disappears.
This indicates that the final term in Eq. (
15
) is the only term involved in correcting correlated shrinkage bias.

Overlapping samples bias occurs because the same or overlapping samples are used to estimate
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
.
When there is no overlap between the units used to estimate the outcome model and those used to estimate the precision model,
the second term in Eq. (
15
) disappears because
n_{\theta\gamma}=|\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}|=0
.
This indicates that the second term is primarily involved in correcting overlapping samples bias.

4
Assumptions

We state the formal assumptions for our main result, Theorem
2.1
. In what follows, we denote by

\mu_{1}(\boldsymbol{A})\geq\mu_{2}(\boldsymbol{A})\geq\dots\geq\mu_{n}(\boldsymbol{A})
the ordered eigenvalues of the symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
.

The assumptions we will use when studying the Lasso estimator are exactly the assumptions used in
[
CMW20
]
, which we repeat here.
A vector
\boldsymbol{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is said to
({\boldsymbol{x}},M)
-
approximately sparse
for
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
and
M>0
if there exists
\bar{\boldsymbol{t}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with
\|\bar{\boldsymbol{t}}-\boldsymbol{t}\|_{1}/\sqrt{p}\leq M
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\operatorname{sign}(\bar{\boldsymbol{t}})
, where the sign is taken in an entry-wise manner:
\operatorname{sign}(x)=1
for
x>0
,
\operatorname{sign}(x)=-1
for
x<0
, and
\operatorname{sign}(x)=\{-1,0,1\}
for
x=0
.

The structural assumption we will make depends on a certain functional notion of Gaussian width provided in
[
CMW20
]
.
We consider the probability space
({\mathbb{R}}^{p},\mathcal{B},\gamma_{p})
where
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\gamma_{p}
is the standard Gaussian measure in
p
dimensions.
Let
L^{2}
be the space of functions
\boldsymbol{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
which are square integrable in
({\mathbb{R}}^{p},\mathcal{B},\gamma_{p})
, and denote by
\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}[\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\xi}),\boldsymbol{f}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\xi})\rangle_{L^{2}}]
the inner product on this space, where
\boldsymbol{\xi}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
. Let
\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{2}}
be the norm induced by this inner product.
For
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{p}
, define

F(\boldsymbol{v};{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}):=\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle+\|({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v})_{S^{c}}\|_{1}\quad\text{for }S:=\mathsf{supp}({\boldsymbol{x}}),

(25)

and define the
functional Gaussian width

\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}):=\sup\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\xi}\rangle_{L^{2}}:\;\;\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2},\;\;\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1,\;\;\mathbb{E}[F(\boldsymbol{v};{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})]\leq 0\Big\},

(26)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}
denotes the identity function of
L^{2}
.
Finally, we can provide the notion of approximate sparsity used in our assumptions for the Lasso.

Definition 4.1
.

We say
\boldsymbol{t}
is
(s,W,M)
-approximately sparse for
W>0
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
if there exists
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\{-1,0,1\}
wuch that
\boldsymbol{t}
is
({\boldsymbol{x}},M)
-approximately sparse,
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|_{0}=s
, and
\mathcal{G}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\leq W
.

We refer the reader to
[
CMW20
]
for a more detailed discussion of the functional Gaussian width, its relation to other notions of Gaussian width, and references to the literature.

A1.
The population covariance has eigenvalues bounded by
0<\mu_{\min}\leq\mu_{j}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\leq\mu_{\max}<\infty
,
the noise variances are bounded by
0<\kappa_{\min}^{2}\leq\kappa^{2}\leq\kappa_{\max}^{2}<\infty
,
0<\sigma_{\min}^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}^{2}<\infty
,
and the parameter of interest is bounded by
|\beta|\leq\beta_{\max}<\infty
.

A2.

Depending on the regression method used, we make the following assumptions (for either
\mathsf{x}\in\{\theta,\gamma\}
):

•

For least-squares regression, we assume
n_{\mathsf{x}}/p\geq\delta_{\mathrm{min}}>1
.

•

For ridge regression, we assume the parameter has bounded
\ell_{2}
-norm
\|\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq r_{\max}^{2}
or
\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq r_{\max}^{2}
,
regularization parameter

0\leq\lambda_{\min}\leq\lambda_{\mathsf{x}}\leq\lambda_{\max}<\infty
, and
n_{\mathsf{x}}/p\geq\delta_{\mathrm{min}}>0
.
We assume
(\delta_{\mathrm{min}}-1)\vee\lambda_{\min}>0
.

•

For the Lasso, we assume the parameter
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(resp.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
) is
(s,\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{x}}/p}(1-\Delta_{\min}),M)
-approximately sparse for some
s/p>\nu_{\min}
,
\Delta_{\min}\in(0,1)
, and
n_{\mathsf{x}}/p\in[\delta_{\min},\delta_{\max}]
(for
\mathsf{x}\in\{\theta,\gamma\}
) for

0<\delta_{\min}\leq\delta_{\max}<\infty
,
and
0<\lambda_{\min}<\lambda_{\mathsf{x}}<\lambda_{\max}<\infty
.

(We emphasize that each bullet point applies only to the model which uses that regression method. For example, if we use least squares for the precision regression and the Lasso for the outcome regression, we assume
n_{\gamma}/p\geq\delta_{\min}
and
n_{\theta}/p\in[\delta_{\min},\delta_{\max}]
.)

We denote the collection of model parameters appearing in assumptions
A1
and
A2
by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, respectively.
That is,

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}=(\mu_{\min},\mu_{\max},\kappa_{\min},\kappa_{\max},\sigma_{\min},\sigma_{\max},\beta_{\max})\text{ and }\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}=(\delta_{\min},\delta_{\max},\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max},\Delta_{\min},M,\nu_{\min}).

(27)

5
Numerical simulations

We carried out a numerical comparison of various debiasing methods for synthetic data under a few data distribution models. We
focus on the case
\mathcal{I}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
, with
n_{\gamma}>p
,
n_{\theta}<p
, so that we can evaluate

\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
using least squares for precision regression.
In this secion, we denote the Lasso regularization parameter in the outcome model as
\lambda:=\lambda_{\theta}
.
Below, we define the data distributions used in simulations in terms of the joint covariance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\overline{p}\times\overline{p}}
,

\overline{p}=p+1
, of
(w_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})
:

Circulant
. The precision matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{-1}
is a circulant matrix, namely

({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{-1})_{ij}=s(i-j)
where it is understood that
s(-k)=s(k)
and
s(\overline{p}+k)=s(k)
for all
k
.
(Note that, as a consequence,
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}
is circulant as well.) As a special example, we consider the following choice:

\displaystyle s(0)=1\,,\;\;\;s(1)=0.4\,,\;\;\;s(2)=0.3\,,\;\;\;s(3)=0.2\,\;\;\;s(k)=0\,\;\forall k\in\{4,\dots,\overline{p}-1\}\,,

(28)

together with the following choice for the parameters vector (with
s_{0}
even):

\displaystyle\beta=\mu\,,\;\;\;\;\theta_{1}=\dots=\theta_{s_{0}/2-1}=\mu\,,\;\;\;\;\theta_{s_{0}/2}=\dots=\theta_{s_{0}-1}=-\mu\,,\;\;\;\;\theta_{i}=0,\;\;\;\forall i\geq s_{0}\,.

(29)

Block model
. The covariance has a block structure with block comprising
\ell_{\textrm{b}}
equicorrelated features.
Namely, we denote by
b(i)=\lfloor i/\ell_{\textrm{b}}\rfloor
the block index for the
i
-th covariate, and set

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if $i=j$,}\\
q/\ell_{\textrm{b}}&\mbox{ if $b(i)=b(j)$ but $i\neq j$,}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(30)

Futher, for
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
s\in\{+1,-1\}
, we consider the following model for the coefficients vector

\displaystyle\beta=\mu\,,\;\;\;\;\theta_{1}=\dots=\theta_{s_{0}-1}=-\mu\,,\;\;\;\;\theta_{i}=0,\;\;\;\forall i\geq s_{0}\,.

(31)

This data distribution is parametrized by the tuple
(q,\ell_{\textrm{b}},\mu,s_{0})
.

For each of these data distributions, we compute various estimators of the parameter of interest
\beta
.
Figure
1
illustrates the dependence on the regularization parameter
\lambda
. The emerging scenario
is fairly robust with respect to the choice of
\lambda
: the CAD estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is nearly unbiased over a broad range of values of
\lambda
,
while all other estimators are biased. The dispersion of these estimators on the other hand depends on the choice of
\lambda
.

We then focus on two specific procedures to choose
\lambda
, described below. These correspond to a semi-realistic scenario in which
\lambda

is not selected following theoretical prescriptions (e.g. the ‘universal’ value
\lambda=\lambda_{\mbox{\tiny\rm MM}}:=\sigma\sqrt{2\log p}
),
and is instead selected to optimize reconstruction on an hold-out set.

Let us emphasize that these procedures are not entirely realistic, in that they makes use of a large hold-out dataset and have access to
the unknown parameter
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, to select the optimal
\lambda
. As such, these should not be regarded as practical methods for selecting
\lambda
,
but rather as ‘oracle’ choices that we use for comparing different debiasing methods in simulations.

Model-size based selection.
We generate
m_{\mbox{\tiny\rm orac}}
datasets
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(i)},{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(i)}
,
i\leq m_{\mbox{\tiny\rm orac}}
from the
same distribution as the original data. In particular,
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times p}
. For each of
these datasets,
we fit the Lasso for a grid of values of the regularization parameter
\lambda\in\Lambda:=\{\lambda_{k}=k\lambda_{\mbox{\tiny\rm MM}}/10:\,k\leq 100\}
,
where
\lambda_{\mbox{\tiny\rm MM}}
is the ‘universal’ regularization value.
We let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}(\lambda)
be the Lasso parameter estimate for regularization
\lambda
,

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}^{(i)}:=\min\big\{\lambda\in\Lambda:\;\;\;\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}(\lambda)\|_{0}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{0}\big\}\,.

(32)

We then set
\lambda
equal to the (empirical)
90\%
percentile of the set of values
(\hat{\lambda}^{(i)})_{i\leq m_{\mbox{\tiny\rm orac}}}
.

This choice of
\lambda
mimics the behavior of a parsimonious statistician who chooses a value
of
\lambda
that produces an approximately correct model size.

Estimation error-based selection.

We proceed as above, except that, for each data set
{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(i)},{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(i)}
, the corresponding optimal
value of
\lambda
is selected via

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}^{(i)}:=\max\big\{\lambda\in\Lambda:\;\;\;\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\lambda)-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}\leq 1.1\min_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\lambda^{\prime})-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|_{2}\big\}\,.

(33)

As above, in our simulations we set
\lambda
equal to the (empirical)
90\%
percentile of the set of values

(\hat{\lambda}^{(i)})_{i\leq m_{\mbox{\tiny\rm orac}}}
.
This choice mimicks the behavior of a statistician that tries to optimize the estimation error.

For experiments in which the precision model
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is also fitted using the Lasso, the same procedure is
repeated to select the corresponding
\lambda
parameter.

Figure 2:
Comparing different debiasing methods, for different data distributions. Left: block model
with
\ell_{\textrm{b}}=10
,
q=1
,
\mu=2
,
\sigma=1
.
Right: circulant model with
\mu=2
,
\sigma=1
and inverse covariance specified in Eq. (
28
).
In both cases,
n_{\theta}=200
,
n_{\gamma}=600
,
p=400
, and we use the model size based selection method for
\lambda
.
Bands correspond to
20\%
and
80\%
percentiles of various debiased estimates over
500
repetitions.

Same as in Figure
2
, except that we use the estimation error-based selection
method for
\lambda
.

except that we use the estimation error-based selection method for
\lambda
.

The results of our simulations are summarized in Figures
2
,
3
and in Tables
1
,
