,
3
,
4
.
The CAD estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is compared with the
simple Lasso estimator
\widehat{\beta}
(see Eq. (
2
)), and
the naive debiased estimators
\widehat{\beta}_{{\mathrm{nv}}}=\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{naive}}
and
\widehat{\beta}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{nv}}}=\widehat{\beta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{naive}}

(see Eqs. (
7
), (
13
)).

For each of
these estimators, we report the empirical mean and standard deviation over
n_{\text{rep}}=10,000
data realizations. The CAD estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
is the only one that has consistently negligible bias. We also report the Kolmogorov-Smirnov
p-value for testing normality of
(\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}-\beta)/{\sf std}(\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}})
. We observe that
{\sf KS}(\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}})
is
most of the time large, indicating that the distribution of
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}
typically cannot be distiguished from normal using
10,000

samples.

6
General theorems on simultaneous regression

The consistency of the CAD estimate (Theorem
2.1
) is a corollary of general theorems on the joint distribution of regression estimators fit using the same (or overlapping) data from two linear models.
These theorems are more powerful than Theorem
2.1

and may be of independent interest.
In this section, we present these general results.

6.1
A symmetric rewriting of the regression models

Mathematically,
there is no reason to think of
{\boldsymbol{y}}
as an outcome variable and
\boldsymbol{w}
as a covariate,
and not the other way around.
We introduce new notation which makes this symmetry explicit.
We will henceforth denote the outcome variables by
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}
, the regression parameters by
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}
, and the noise variables by
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
,
with
k=1
for the precision model and
k=2
for the outcome model.
Explicitly, set

{\boldsymbol{y}}_{1}:=\boldsymbol{w}
,
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{2}:={\boldsymbol{y}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}:={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}:=\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}:=\kappa\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp}
,

\boldsymbol{e}_{2}:=\kappa\beta\boldsymbol{w}^{\perp}+\sigma\boldsymbol{z}
.
The precision and outcome models can be written as

{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}={\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+\boldsymbol{e}_{k},\quad k=1,2,

(34)

where
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})\sim\mathsf{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{S}_{e}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N}\big)
,
with

\boldsymbol{S}_{e}:=\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{e_{1}}^{2}&\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}\\
\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}&\tau_{e_{2}}^{2},\end{pmatrix}

and
\tau_{e_{1}}^{2}=\kappa^{2}
,
\tau_{e_{2}}^{2}=\kappa^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma^{2}
, and
\rho_{e}=\kappa\beta/(\kappa^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma^{2})^{1/2}
.
For reasons which will become clear, we will refer to the model in Eq. (
35
) as the
random-design model
.
Succinctly,
we can write it as

\begin{gathered}\textbf{Random-design model}\\
\boldsymbol{Y}={\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}+\boldsymbol{E},\end{gathered}

(35)

where
\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times 2}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times 2}
,
and these matrices have columns
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
,
k=1,2
.
We will also denote the quantities
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\gamma},\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{\theta}
appearing in the definition of
r_{i}^{\gamma},r_{i}^{\theta}

and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\mathrm{d}}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}
(see Eqs. (
12
) and (
14
)) as
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
for
k=1,2
.
We will see that
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
concentrates on a deterministic quantity
\mathsf{df}_{k}
,
whose definition we postpone Eqs. (
41
), (
42
), and the discussion after Eq. (
43
).
The quantity
\mathsf{df}_{k}
which we define is non-empirical: although she can estimate it, she cannot compute its exact value using information she know.
Nevertheless, it is most convenient to develop theory for the debiased regression estiamtes and degrees of freedom adjusted residuals using this deterministic quantity in place of
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
, and later show that we may replace
\mathsf{df}_{k}
with the estimate
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
without impacting our results.
Thus, we will study regression estimators in the model (
35
) after replacing
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
by
\mathsf{df}_{k}
.
We write these as

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2n_{k}}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\Omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+\frac{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})}{n_{k}-\mathsf{df}_{k}},

(36)

where
n_{k},\mathcal{I}_{k},\Omega_{k}
are defined in the obvious way.
We also define
n_{12}:=|\mathcal{I}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{2}|

and define the matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times 2}
to have columns
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}
,
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}
to have columns
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
.
Note that there is some abuse in terminology in calling
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
an “estimator” because it depends on the non-empirical quantity
\mathsf{df}_{k}
.
We will show that all of our results hold also with
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in place of
\mathsf{df}_{k}
(see Theorem
6.2
(iii)).

Although it is typical to think of the task in the regression model (
35
) as parameter estimation,
it is enlightening to think of the task as also including noise estimation.
The tasks are complementary: the better we estimate the unknown parameter, the better we can estimate the noise, and vice versa.
In the regression procedure (
36
),
the fitted residuals are natural estimates of the noise,
and
the degrees-of-freedom adjusted residuals can be understood as their debiased counterparts.
In particular,
for noise estimation,
we write

\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}}=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k},\\
\boldsymbol{0}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c},\end{cases}\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}=\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}-\mathsf{df}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}+({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{X}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}).

(37)

Although it may seem unnatural to set the out-of-sample noise estimate
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
the proofs of our main theorems will make clear why this is—for our purposes—the correct choice.
We define the matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times 2}
to have columns
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}
,
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}^{\mathrm{d}}
to have columns
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
.
As with the debiased estimate of the parameter,
there is some abuse in terminology in calling
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
an “estimator” because it depends on the non-empirical quantity
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
.
As we have already stated, we will show that all of our results hold also with
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in place of
\mathsf{df}_{k}
(see Theorem
6.2
(iii)).

6.2
Joint characterization for simultaneous regression

The proof of Theorem
2.1
is an application of a more general theorem characterterizing the joint behavior of the regression estimators
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}\}_{k}
,
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}_{k}
,
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\}_{k}
, and
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}_{k}
.

To state the result,
we must first introduce two new statistical models—one for parameter estimation and one for noise estimation—which behave, in a certain sense, like the random design model (
35
).
The two models are

\begin{gathered}\textbf{Fixed-design model}\\
\textbf{for parameter estimation}\\
\boldsymbol{Y}^{f}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}+\boldsymbol{G}^{f}\;\;\text{(FD-P)}\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}\textbf{Fixed-design model}\\
\textbf{for noise estimation}\\
\boldsymbol{R}^{f}=\boldsymbol{E}+\boldsymbol{H}^{f}\;\;\text{(FD-N)}\end{gathered}

(38)

where
\boldsymbol{G}^{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times 2}
has columns
(\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f},\boldsymbol{g}_{2}^{f})\sim\mathsf{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{S}_{g}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\big)
,
and
\boldsymbol{H}^{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times 2}
has columns
(\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{f},\boldsymbol{h}_{2}^{f})\sim\mathsf{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{S}_{h}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N}\big)
,
for some matrices
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\boldsymbol{S}_{h}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{2}
to be chosen.
Like model (
35
),
the fixed-design model for parameter estimation involves
linear measurements of the unknown parameters
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}
with correlated Gaussian noise.
The models differ in that the random design matrix
{\boldsymbol{X}}
in model (
35
) is replaced by the fixed design matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}
in (FD-P). (A consequence is that the dimensionality of the observations is
p
instead of
N
).
The fixed-design model for noise estimation also has similarities with model (
35
).
Indeed,
{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}\sim\mathsf{N}(\boldsymbol{0},(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\boldsymbol{\Theta})\otimes\mathbf{I}_{N})
,
so that model (
35
) can be viewed as specifying correlated Gaussian observations of the noise variables.
In this sense, the model (
35
) takes the same form as (FD-N).
The models differ in that in model (
35
) the matrix
{\boldsymbol{X}}
is observed,
providing information about the Gaussian corruption which is not available in (FD-N).

Our general characterizaton result establishes a precise form of the following claim:

Parameter estimation in model (
35
) behaves like parameter estimation in model
(FD-P)
.

Noise estimation in model (
35
) behaves like noise estimation in model
(FD-N)
.

The correctness of these statements relies on specifying the correct estimators and covariance structures
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
in the fixed-design models.

In analogy with Eq. (
36
),
the parameter estimates in the fixed-design model (FD-P) are

\begin{gathered}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}:=\eta_{k}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{f};\zeta_{k}):=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{k}}\Omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f})={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{f}.\end{gathered}

(39)

For noise estimation, the analogy to Eq. (
37
) is less clear.
We set the noise estimates in the fixed-design model (FD-N) to be

\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{f}_{k,\mathcal{I}}=\begin{cases}\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{r}^{f}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k},\\
\boldsymbol{0}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c},\end{cases}\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}=\frac{1-\zeta_{k}}{\zeta_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}+\Big(\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{f}-\frac{1-\zeta_{k}}{\zeta_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}\Big)=\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{f}.

(40)

The proofs of our main theorems will make clear why this is—for our purposes—the correct choice of noise estimates.

The covariance structures
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
and parameters
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
are determined by
a system of equations which we call the
fixed-point equations
.
The system of equations involves functions describing the behavior of parameter estimation in the fixed-design model.
The functions are

\begin{gathered}\textbf{Second moments of prediction error}\\
\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{f}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{f}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})],\\
\phantom{k=1,2}\end{gathered}\qquad\begin{gathered}\textbf{Degrees-of-freedom}\\
\mathsf{df}_{k}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{div}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\eta_{k}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{f};\zeta_{k})],\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad k=1,2.\end{gathered}

(41)

The right-hand sides depend implicitly on
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
via
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{f},\boldsymbol{Y}^{f}
.
We define

\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}

as solutions to the system of equations

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{S}_{g}=\boldsymbol{N}^{-}\odot\big(\boldsymbol{S}_{e}+\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})\big),\\
\zeta_{k}=1-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})}{n_{k}},\quad k=1,2,\end{gathered}

(42)

where
\boldsymbol{N}^{-}:=\begin{pmatrix}1/n_{1}&n_{12}/(n_{1}n_{2})\\
n_{12}/(n_{1}n_{2})&1/n_{2}\end{pmatrix}
and
\odot
denotes coordinate-wise multiplication.
These equations have a solution.

Lemma 6.1

(Existence and uniqueness of fixed-point parameters)
.

Assume
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is invertible,
\boldsymbol{S}_{e}\succeq 0
,
and for each
k
, either
n_{k}>p
or
\lambda_{k}>0
, where
\lambda_{k}
is the regularization parameter of the penalty (see Eq. (
9
)).
Then equations (
42
) have a unique solution
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}
.

Lemma
6.1
is proved in Section
M.1
.

Given a solution
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}
to Eqs. (
42
),
define the parameters

\boldsymbol{S}_{h}:=\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}),\qquad\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{df}_{1}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})&0\\
0&\mathsf{df}_{2}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})\end{pmatrix}.

(43)

For simplicity, we will often denote
\mathsf{df}_{k}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})
by
\mathsf{df}_{k}
,
where it is understood that this is evaluated at solutions to the fixed point equations (
42
).

We are ready to state our general characterization result.

Theorem 6.2

(Joint characterization)
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
hold. Let
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}
be the unique solutions of the
fixed-point equations (
42
) and
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
be defined by Eq. (
43
).
Define the fixed design model and estimators according to Eqs.
38
,
39
,
40
.

There exist constants
\nu,c^{\prime}>0
and functions
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
which depend uniquely on

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and the regression method such that
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)\geq c^{\prime}(\epsilon^{\nu}\wedge 1)
and the
following occurs.

1.

(Noise estimation)

Let
\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)
be a real-valued function which is
M_{k}
-Lipschitz in its arguments
\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}/\sqrt{n_{2}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}/\sqrt{n_{2}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
for
k=1,2
.
Then
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}

with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d},f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)\Big]\Big|<(M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{2}}+M_{2})\,\epsilon.\end{gathered}

(44)

The result is also true if we replace
\mathcal{I}_{2}
with
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
n_{2}
with
n_{1}
,
and
M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{2}}+M_{2}
with
M_{1}+M_{2}\sqrt{p/n_{1}}
.

2.

(Parameter estimation)

Let
\phi_{\theta}:({\mathbb{R}}^{p})^{4}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be
M_{1}
-Lipschitz in its first two arguments and
M_{2}
-Lipschitz in its second two arguments.
Then
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{f,\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\big)\big]\big|<\min\big\{M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{1}}+M_{2},M_{1}+M_{2}\sqrt{p/n_{2}}\big\}\,\epsilon.\end{gathered}

(45)

3.

Parts (i) holds with
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in place of
\mathsf{df}_{k}
in the definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
(see Eq. (
37
))
if the right-hand side of the bound is changed to
(M_{1}(\sqrt{p/n_{2}}+p/n_{1})+M_{2})\epsilon
,
(or if we replace
\mathcal{I}_{2}
with
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, the upper bound becomes
(M_{1}+M_{2}(\sqrt{p/n_{1}}+p/n_{2}))\epsilon
).

Parts (ii) holds (with the same bound on the right-hand side) with
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in place of
\mathsf{df}_{k}
in the definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
(see Eq. (
36
)).

Remark 6.3
.

We conjecture that Theorem
6.2
holds for
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)=C
and
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)=c\epsilon^{2}
.
Our proof techniques allow us to establish the result for
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)=c\epsilon^{\nu}
for some
\nu>2
.
Because the exponent
\nu
we can provide is not the correct one and tracking it complicates our proofs,
we do not track it.
We further conjecture that the upper bound on the right-hand sides can be replaced by
(M_{1}+M_{2})\sqrt{p/n_{2}}\,\epsilon
in the case of noise estimation,
and by
(M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{1}}+M_{2}\sqrt{p/n_{2}})\epsilon
in the case of parameter estimation.
See the remarks following Lemma
E.1
for further interpretation of these rates.

6.3
Interpretation of the joint characterization

As anticipated, Theorem
6.2
states that parameter estimation in model (
35
) behaves like parameter estimation in model (FD-P),
and noise estimation in model (
35
) behaves like noise estimation in model (FD-N),
where “behaves like” refers to the concentration properties of Lipschitz functions.
Because
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}
are
C
-Lipschitz functions of the Gaussian noise
\boldsymbol{G}^{f},\boldsymbol{H}^{f}
,
in the fixed design models the functions
\phi_{e},\phi_{\theta}
will concentrate on their expectations.
Theorem
6.2
states that
\phi_{e},\phi_{\theta}
also concentrate in the random-design model,
and they concentrate on the same values.

Theorem
6.2
gives substantive meaning to the fixed point parameters
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
:
they are the values on which
the random-design quantities

\frac{1}{p}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{n_{k}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}})^{\top}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{E}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}})

(46)

concentrate.
Although the quantities in the previous display are not Lipschitz functions of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}^{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}
,
their concentration is straightforward to establish using a Lipschitz approximation argument,
which we will carry out formally in our proofs when necessary (see Section
G
).
Theorem
6.2
can also be used to establish the concentration of several other quantities in the regression models.
An important example is the random design quantity
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})
,
which concentrates on
\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
.
The quantity
\mathsf{R}_{kk}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\boldsymbol{S}_{h,kk}
can then be interpreted as the typical prediction risk of the estimate from regression
k
,
and
\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})
as a measure of the typical alignment between the errors in the predictions from the two regressions,
which the reader will recall gives the bias of the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{split}}
(see Section
3
).

An implication of Theorem
6.2
is that the random-design quantities which concentrate on
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
,
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
, and
\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})
will, with high-probability, approximately satisfy Eq. (
42
).
This has important statistical consequences.
Indeed,
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
are not known a priori to the statistician,
and they describe the behavior of the errors (
46
) and
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})
which the statistician does not observe but may wish to estimate.
As we will see,
the fixed point equations (
6.1
) will allow us to write
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
,
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
, and other unknown quantities in terms of parameters we can estimate from data.
For example, Theorem
6.2
can be used to show that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}/n_{k}
concentrates on
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}]/n_{k}=\boldsymbol{S}_{e}+\boldsymbol{S}_{h}=\boldsymbol{S}_{e}+\mathsf{R}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})
.
The fixed point equations (
42
) then suggest that

\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{g}^{(k)}:=\boldsymbol{N}^{-}\odot\Big(\frac{1}{n_{k}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}\Big),

(47)

is a consistent estimator of
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
,
and thus of the error structure of the debiased estimates.
We will see that this claim is correct.
Pushing this strategy further, we will develop a consistent estimator of the noise covariance
\boldsymbol{S}_{e}
,
which
will give us Theorem
2.1
.

For future reference, we have collected in Table
5
several random design quantities and the “typical values” on which they concentrate.
For some of these quantities,
we have provided multiple equivalent ways of writing the typical values,
either using the fixed-point equations or the definitions of the fixed point parameters.
We have also introduced the parameter
\boldsymbol{S}_{\hat{e}^{d}}:=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f,\mathrm{d}\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}]/n_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{v}:=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{f}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{f}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})]
.
This is consistent with a convention which we adopt by which
\boldsymbol{S}
always denotes the fixed-design second-moment matrix of the quantity in its subscript (possibly with some renormalization).
We will later define
\boldsymbol{V}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})
, justifying the notation
\boldsymbol{S}_{v}
.
We do not claim to have yet proved any of the concentration properties or relationships implied by Table
5
(although they are straightforward to show using Theorem
6.2
).
These will be provided in our proofs as needed.
Table
5
is not intended as a replacement for a proof, but rather as a useful reference for the reader.

6.4
Estimating noise covariance and the proof of Theorem
2.1

In this section,
we construct a consistent estimate of the noise covariance
\boldsymbol{S}_{e}
,
and then show that the consistency of the CAD estimate (Theorem
2.1
) follows from this construction.
Our estimate of
\boldsymbol{S}_{e}
is a generalization of the estimate of the Lasso noise
level provided by
[
BEM13
]
(see Eq. (1.4) and the following display in that paper).
However, the setting here is considerably more challenging since we study two coupled
linear models instead of a single one, and correlated Gaussian designs instead of iid designs
(
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\neq\mathbf{I}_{p}
).

In order to derive our estimate of
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
,
we follow the strategy described in the previous section:
we use the characterizations listed in Table
5

and the fixed point equations (
42
) to find a quantity which concentrates on
\boldsymbol{S}_{e}
.
We will first derive the estimate heuristically, and then will prove its consistency.

Writing
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})-(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}-\boldsymbol{\Theta})
and using Table
5
,
we expect that
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}})
will concentrate on

p\boldsymbol{S}_{g}-\boldsymbol{S}_{g}\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}-\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}\boldsymbol{S}_{g}+\boldsymbol{S}_{h}=p\boldsymbol{S}_{g}-\boldsymbol{S}_{g}\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}-\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}\boldsymbol{S}_{g}+\boldsymbol{S}_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}-\boldsymbol{S}_{e}.

(48)

We proposed in Eq. (
47
) an estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{g}^{(k)}
of
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
.
A natural estimate of
\boldsymbol{S}_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}
is

\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{(k)}:=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}.

(49)

Combining these estimates and identities,
we propose the estimate

\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{e}^{(k)}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{(k)}+p\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{g}^{(k)}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{g}^{(k)}\widehat{\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{g}^{(k)}-(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}),

(50)

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{D\hskip-2.84544ptf}}=\operatorname{diag}(\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{1},\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{2})
.
Our main theorem is as follows:

Theorem 6.4
.

Consider that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
are computed with
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in place of
\mathsf{df}_{k}
in Eqs. (
36
) and (
37
).
Under assumptions
A1
and
A2
,
there exist constants
\nu,c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that

\mathsf{c}(\varepsilon)>c^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\nu}
and, for
\epsilon\leq c^{\prime}
,

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{e}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{S}_{e}\big\|_{\mathsf{F}}>C^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}\wedge n_{2}}}\,\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}.\end{gathered}

(51)

We prove Theorem
6.4
in Section
K
.

As a byproduct, Theorem
6.4
provides an estimate of the noise level

\sigma
, which generalizes the estimate in
[
BEM13
]
, which was limited
to uncorrelated designs.
Recall that
S_{e,kk}=\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}
is the noise level in the
k^{\text{th}}
linear model.
The estimate for
\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}
provided by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{S}}_{e}^{(k)}
is

\hat{\tau}_{e_{k}}^{2}:=\Big(1+\frac{p}{n_{k}}-2\frac{\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}}{n_{k}}\Big)\frac{\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\mathrm{d}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}.

(52)

In the case of the Lasso,
we estimate
\mathsf{df}_{k}
by
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}=\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0}
.
Further, when
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
,
we have
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}={\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})/(n_{k}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0})
.
Finally, the degrees of freedom adjusted residuals
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}

are given by
({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})/(1-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0}/n_{k})
(see Eq. (
37
)).
Thus, in the special case of the Lasso with
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
, we may rewrite the estimate above as

\hat{\tau}_{e_{k}}^{2}=\Big(1+\frac{p}{n_{k}}-2\frac{\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0}}{n_{k}}\Big)\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}(1-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0}/n_{k})^{2}}-\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})\|_{2}^{2}}{(n_{k}-\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{0})^{2}},

(53)

which agrees, under our normalization, with the estimate provided in
[
BEM13
]
.

As a sanity check, it is useful to write the noise estimate
\hat{\tau}_{e_{k}}^{2}
in the case of OLS when
n_{k}>p
,
a case to which Theorem
6.4
also applies.
In this case,
\mathsf{df}_{k}=p
and
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})=0
.
The noise estimate simplifies to

\hat{\tau}_{e_{k}}^{2}=\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}-p}\quad\text{for estimation by least squares.}

(54)

This is the standard unbiased estimate of noise variance with least squares.

We can now prove Theorem
2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Using that
\tau_{e_{1}}^{2}=\kappa^{2}
,
\tau_{e_{2}}^{2}=\kappa^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma^{2}
, and
\rho_{e}=\kappa\beta/(\kappa^{2}\beta^{2}+\sigma^{2})^{1/2}
,
we see that
\beta=\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}/\tau_{e_{1}}^{2}=S_{e,12}/S_{e,11}
.
Using that
r_{i}^{\gamma}=e_{1,i}^{\mathrm{d}}
,
r_{i}^{\theta}=e_{2,i}^{\mathrm{d}}
, and
\hat{\kappa}^{2}=\widehat{S}_{e,11}
,
we see that
\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{cad}}=\widehat{S}_{e,12}/\widehat{S}_{e,11}
.
Theorem
2.1
follows by apply Theorem
6.4
and the
\delta
-method, using that
S_{e,12}
is bounded above and
S_{e,11}
is bounded below and above by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
-dependent constants.
∎

Appendix A
Convention on using constants
C,c,\mathsf{C}(\epsilon),\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)

Throughout the appendices, the constants
C,C^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}>0
and

\mathsf{C}(\epsilon),\mathsf{c}(\epsilon):{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
(or these quantities
with subscripts) are always assumed be positive, finite, and depend only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
and the regression methods used. Unless otherwise stated, these may change at each appearance.
Further, the exponent
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)
is understood to satisfy
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)>c^{\prime}(\varepsilon^{\nu}\wedge 1)

for some constant
\nu<\infty
that also can change from line to line.
These conventions will usually not be repeated throughout the text.

We use these conventions to make several statements more concise.
For example, the statement “we conclude
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}>c
” should be interpreted to mean “we conclude
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
is bounded below by a positive constant depending only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
, and the regression methods used,” although it will usually not be stated in this more unwieldy way.

Appendix B
The
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso

For technical reasons, it is difficult to prove Theorem
6.2
directly for the Lasso estimator.
In particular,
the non-differentiability of the
\ell_{1}
-norm leads to technical challenges in characterizing the behavior of the debiased Lasso
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
.
To address these challenges, we use a smoothing technique, which was also used in
[
CMW20
]
.

We introduce a regression estimator which uses a smoothed version of the
\ell_{1}
-norm.
The
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso with smoothing parameter
\alpha_{k}>0
and regularization
\lambda_{k}
uses the penalty
\Omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi}):=\lambda_{k}\mathsf{M}_{\alpha_{k}/\sqrt{n_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{\pi})/\sqrt{n_{k}}
, where

\mathsf{M}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\pi}):=\inf_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2t}\|\boldsymbol{\pi}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{1}\Big\}.

(55)

For
t=0
, we define
\mathsf{M}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\pi})=\|\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{1}
.
It is straightforward to check that for all
\alpha_{k}>0
,
\nabla\mathsf{M}_{\alpha_{k}/\sqrt{n_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{\pi})/\sqrt{n_{k}}
exists and is

1/\alpha_{k}
-Lipschitz.
We add to assumption
A2
the following assumptions for the case that the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso is used:

A2
(continued)
If we use the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso, we make the following assumption:

•

We assume the parameter
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
(resp.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
) is
(s,\sqrt{n_{k}/p}(1-\Delta_{\min}),M)
-approximately sparse for some
s/p>\nu_{\min}
,
\Delta_{\min}\in(0,1)
, and
n_{k}/p\in[\delta_{\min},\delta_{\max}]
for
0<\delta_{\min}\leq\delta_{\max}<\infty
,
and
0<\lambda_{\min}<\lambda_{k}<\lambda_{\max}<\infty
.
We assume
\alpha_{k}<\alpha_{\max}
.

We include in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
the constant
\alpha_{\max}
which appears in our assumption about the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso.
We will prove the joint-characterization under assumption
A1
and
A2
even when the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso is used for one or both of the regressions.
In the remainder of the paper, we will develop results for the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso alongside our development of results for OLS, ridge-regression, and the Lasso.

Appendix C
The fixed-point parameters

Our proofs will require certain bounds on and identities satisfied by the fixed point parameters
\boldsymbol{S}_{g}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
.
They will sometimes benefit from new notation for references entries of these matrices.
The purpose of this section is to provide these results and new notation.

C.1
Non-matricial form of fixed-point parameters

For any matrix
\boldsymbol{S}
, possibly with subscripts,
we will denote the variances and correlations implied by the covariance structure
\boldsymbol{S}
by
\tau^{2}
and
\rho
, respectively, with the corresponding subscripts.
For example,
we will denote

\boldsymbol{S}_{g}=:\begin{pmatrix}\tau_{g_{1}}^{2}&\tau_{g_{1}}\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}\\
\tau_{g_{1}}\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}&\tau_{g_{2}}^{2}\end{pmatrix},

(56)

and will similarly define the parameters
\tau_{h_{k}}^{2},\rho_{h},\tau_{\hat{e}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{2},\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}
.
We will also denote
\rho^{\perp}=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
, with appropriate subscripts.
For example,
\rho_{e}^{\perp}=\sqrt{1-\rho_{e}^{2}}
, and so on.

It will frequently be useful to reference individual entries of the fixed-point equations (
42
).
Our new notation facilitates this.
The diagonal equations in Eq. (
42
) are

\begin{gathered}\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\big(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\zeta_{k})\big),\\
\zeta_{k}=1-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}(\tau_{k}^{2},\zeta_{k})}{n_{k}},\end{gathered}

(57)

where
\mathsf{R}_{k}(\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\zeta_{k}):=\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\big]
.
The first equation correpsonds to the
k^{\text{th}}
diagonal entry of the first (matricial) fixed point equation (
42
).
The right-hand side depends on
\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}
via
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}\big]
.
There is some abuse of notation in writing
\mathsf{df}_{k}
as a function of
\tau_{k}^{2},\zeta_{k}
rather than
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{l}\}
.
This is because
\mathsf{df}_{k}
depends on
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{l}\}
only via
\tau_{k}^{2},\zeta_{k}
.

We also have equation

\tau_{g_{1}}\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}=\frac{n_{12}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\big(\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})\big),

(58)

where

\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}):=\mathbb{E}[\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{f,\mathrm{d}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f,\mathrm{d}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}].

(59)

This equation corresponds to the off diagonal entry of the first (matricial) fixed point equation (
42
).
The equations (
57
) and (
58
) are a rewriting of Eq. (
42
).

C.2
Identities satisfied by the fixed-point parameters

The fixed-point parameters satisfy several identities which will be useful in the proofs.
These follow by straightforward algebra from the fixed point equations (
42
) (or, equivalently, Eqs. (
57
) and (
58
)) and the structure of the fixed design models (FD-P) and (FD-N).
For future reference, we collect these identities here.

The variance of the noise in the random-design model satisfies

\begin{gathered}\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}}\|_{2}^{2}]/|\mathcal{I}|,\\
\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}=\mathbb{E}[\langle\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}},\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}}\rangle^{2}]/|\mathcal{I}|,\end{gathered}

(60)

for any subset of indices
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
.

The variance of the noise in the fixed-design model for noise estimation satisfies

\begin{gathered}\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{h}_{k,\mathcal{I}}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}]/|\mathcal{I}|=\mathsf{R}_{k}(\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\zeta_{k})=\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}],\\
\tau_{h_{1}}\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h}=\mathbb{E}[\langle\boldsymbol{h}_{1,\mathcal{I}}^{f},\boldsymbol{h}_{2,\mathcal{I}}^{f}\rangle]/|\mathcal{I}|,=\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\mathbb{E}[\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}\rangle_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}]\end{gathered}

(61)

for any subset of indices
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
.

The variance of the noise in the fixed-design model for parameter estimation satisfies

\begin{gathered}\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{g}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}]/p=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\big(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\zeta_{k})\big)=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\big(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\tau_{\hat{e}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{2},\\
\tau_{g_{1}}\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{1},\boldsymbol{g}_{2}\rangle]}{p}=\frac{n_{12}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\big(\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})\big)=\frac{n_{12}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\big(\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\tau_{h_{1}}\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h}\big)=\frac{n_{12}}{n_{1}n_{2}}\tau_{\hat{e}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}}\tau_{\hat{e}_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}}\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}},\\
\rho_{g}=\frac{n_{12}}{\sqrt{n_{1}n_{2}}}\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}.\end{gathered}

(62)

The variance of the debiased estimates in the fixed-design model for noise estimation satisfies

\begin{gathered}\tau_{\hat{e}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\|_{2}^{2}]/|\mathcal{I}|=\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\mathsf{R}_{k}(\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\zeta_{k})=\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2},\\
\tau_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}}\tau_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}_{2}}\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}=\mathbb{E}[\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}}^{f,\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\rangle]/|\mathcal{I}|=\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\mathsf{R}_{12}(\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\})=\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\tau_{h_{1}}\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h}.\end{gathered}

(63)

The residuals in the fixed design model are written as
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}
.
They satisfy
\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}^{f}=0
and also

\begin{gathered}\frac{1}{n_{k}}\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}]=\zeta_{k}^{2}(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})=\zeta_{k}^{2}\tau_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}_{k}}^{2}=n_{k}\zeta_{k}^{2}\tau_{g_{k}}^{2},\\
\mathbb{E}[\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{f},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{f}\rangle]=n_{12}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}(\tau_{e_{1}}\tau_{e_{2}}\rho_{e}+\tau_{h_{1}}\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h})=n_{12}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\tau_{\hat{e}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}}\tau_{\hat{e}_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}}\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}=n_{1}n_{2}\tau_{g_{1}}\tau_{g_{2}}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\rho_{g},\\
\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}]=n_{12}\zeta_{1}^{2}(\tau_{e_{1}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{1}}^{2})=n_{12}\zeta_{1}^{2}\tau_{\hat{e}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{2}=n_{12}n_{1}\zeta_{1}^{2}\tau_{g_{1}}^{2}.\end{gathered}

(64)

C.3
Bounds on the fixed-point parameters

We will require the following lower and upper bounds on the fixed point parameters.

Lemma C.1

(Bounds on fixed-point parameters)
.

For
k=1,2
,
there exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and regression method dependent constants
C,c>0
(which may change at each appearance) such that
such that

c/n_{k}<\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}<C/n_{k}
,

c<\zeta_{k}\leq 1
,

cp/n_{k}<\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}<Cp/n_{k}
,

c<\tau_{\hat{e}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}}^{2}<C
,
and
|\rho_{g}|,|\rho_{h}|,|\rho_{e}|,|\rho_{\hat{e}^{\mathrm{d}}}|<1-c
.
Further,
\mathsf{df}_{k}/p\leq 1
.

The proofs of Lemma
C.1
is provided in Section
M.1
.

Remark C.2
.

In addition to providing important technical results required for our proofs,
Lemma
C.1
is interesting in its own right because it shows how the fixed point parameters scale with
p
and
n_{k}
.
Most existing exact characterization results either consider an asymptotic limit in
n_{k}/p\rightarrow\delta
or assume that
n_{k}/p
is upper or lower bounded by a constant.
In such results,
n_{k}/p
can be replaced by a constant in upper and lower bounds.
Because we do not assume
n_{k}/p
is bounded above in the case of ridge regression or OLS,
such replacement cannot in general be made, and tracking the dependence of the fixed point parameters on
n_{k}
and
p
will be necessary.
For example, it establishes that
\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}=\Theta(1/n_{k})
,
indicating that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
provides—on average across coordinates—an observation of each coordinate at the parametric rate.
It also establishes that
\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}=\Theta(p/n_{k})
, indicating (recall Table
5
) that the prediction error for each regression is, with high probability,
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\|_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}^{2}=\Theta(p/n_{k})
.
In the proportional regime, this is what we expect.

Appendix D
Proof of joint characterization (Theorem
6.2
(i) and (ii))

In this section, we prove Theorem
6.2
(i) and (ii),
deferring several technical details to later appendices.
We prove prove the extension to empirical
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
(i.e., Theorem
6.2
(iii)) in Section
H
.

We prove Theorem
6.2
(i) and (ii) by
bounding the differences to an intermediate quantity that depends on only one of the regression models at a time.
The intermediate quantity is a function of the vectors
2
2

2

Later in the proof, we will analyze the behavior of the regression problem conditional on
\mathsf{Cond}_{k}
, justifying our choice of notation.

\mathsf{Cond}_{k}:=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}),\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{p})^{2}\times({\mathbb{R}}^{N})^{4}.

(65)

In fact, it will depend on this vectors only via two random vectors

\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{k}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{k}(\mathsf{Cond}_{k})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{k}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{k}(\mathsf{Cond}_{k})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

which are deterministic functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{k}
.
The definition of these functions is complicated and non-intuitive,
and is carefully chosen to make our proofs work.
We postpone providing their definitions to Section
F
(see Eq. (
143
)).
For now,
the reader should think of these quantities as approximating the errors of the debiased estimates in the first regression model.
Indeed:

Lemma D.1
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.

There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
,
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\big\|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})\big\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,\qquad\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\big\|\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-(\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}})\big\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon.

(66)

We prove Lemma
D.1
in Section
J.1
.

Let
\phi_{e},\phi_{\theta}
and
M_{1},M_{2}
be as in Theorem
6.2
.
The intermediate quantities are

\begin{gathered}\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1}):=\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{f,\mathrm{d}})\bigm|\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\big],\\
\phi_{e|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1}):=\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big)\Bigm|\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},\,\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\Big].\end{gathered}

(67)

In this display, the expectations are taken in the fixed-design model, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}
, which are functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
, are considered fixed.
For clarity and with some abuse of notation, we have written each argument passed to
\phi_{e}
explicitly rather than use that set-notation as in Theorem
6.2
.
This is because some of the arguments come from the fixed-design model, and some (which are functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
), come from the random design model.
We bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d},f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)\Big]\Big|>(M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{2}}+M_{2})\epsilon\Big)

(68)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)-\phi_{e|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\Big|>\frac{M_{2}}{2}\,\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d},f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)\Big]\Big|>\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{2}}}+\frac{M_{2}}{2}\Big)\,\epsilon\Big),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\}\big)\big]\Big|>(M_{1}\sqrt{p/n_{1}}+M_{2})\epsilon\Big)

(69)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}\big)-\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\Big|>\frac{1}{2}\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\Big)\,\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\}\big)\big]\Big|>\frac{1}{2}\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\Big)\,\epsilon\Big).

The next two lemmas bound the terms in this decomposition.

Lemma D.2
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.

There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\}\big)\big]\Big|>\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{2}}}\;\Big)\,\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d},f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)\Big]\Big|>\big(M_{1}+M_{2}\big)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{2}}}\,\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}.\end{gathered}

(70)

Lemma D.3

(Conditional characterization)
.

Assume
A1
and
\mathsf{A2}
are satisfied.

There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\}\big)-\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\Big|\geq\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\;\Big)\,\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)-\phi_{e|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\Big|\geq M_{2}\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}.\end{gathered}

(71)

Lemma
D.2
involves only the distribution of objects from the first regression.
To bound it,
we will use a characterization result analogous to Theorem
6.2
which applies to one regression at a time.
We call this the
marginal characterization
.
It is stated in Section
E
and proved in Section
E.2
.
To prove Lemma
D.3
,
we study the distribution of the estimators from the second regression conditional on
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
.
We will use a characterization result analogous to Theorem
6.2

which applies to one of the regressions conditionally on the other.
We call this the
conditional characterization
.
It is stated in Section
F
and proved in Section
F.3
.

By assumption
A2
,
p/n_{2}\leq C
for some
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and regression-method dependent
C
, whence, after adjusting constants, Lemma
D.2
allows us to bound the second terms in the decompositions above by
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
.
Lemma
D.3
allows us to bound the first terms in the decompositions above by
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
.
Combining these bounds, we conclude Theorem
6.2
(i) and (ii).

Appendix E
Marginal characterization

In this section we state and prove the marginal characterization, which describes the behavior of one regression at a time.
Our primary application of the marginal characterization is to prove Lemma
D.2
.
We postpone this proof of Lemma
D.2
to Section
J.2
.

E.1
Statement of marginal characterization

The next lemma states the marginal characterization.

Lemma E.1
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.

1.

There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
and 1-Lipschitz
\phi_{\theta}:({\mathbb{R}}^{p})^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
,
\phi_{e}:({\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}})^{4}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\big)\big]\big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,\\
\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Big]\Big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,\end{gathered}

(72)

where the fixed design model and estimators are defined with
\boldsymbol{S}_{g},\{\zeta_{k}\}
satisfying the fixed-point equations (
42
) and
\boldsymbol{S}_{h}
is defined by Eq. (
43
).

2.

The same holds if we replace
\mathsf{df}_{k}
by
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
in the definitions of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}
in Eqs. (
36
) and (
37
),
where
\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}
is defined by Eq. (
11
).

We provide several remarks on Lemma
E.1
. They are not essential to our exposition and may be skipped.

Remark E.2

(Relation to existing literature)
.

Marginal characterization results are now standard in the literature
[
BM11
,
Sto13
,
TOH15b
,
SC19
,
DW18
,
MM21
,
GAK20
,
CMW20
]
.
Lemma
E.1
differs from previous results in a couple respects.
Most results are typically proved assuming an upper bound on
n_{k}/p
.
In the case of OLS and ridge regression,
we assume no such upper bound on
n_{k}/p
,
and the characterization provided by Lemma
E.1
provides better concentration as
n_{k}/p
gets large.
Establishing the correct dependence on
n_{k}/p
for
n_{k}/p
large requires a more refined argument than existing results in the literature.
We expect the same dependence on
n_{k}/p
to hold for the Lasso and
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso, but do not pursue this in the present paper.
(Recall that for the Lasso, assumption
A2
imposes an upper bound on
n_{k}/p
).

Further, as far as we are aware,
in the case of ridge regression, a characterization result along the lines of Lemma
E.1
has not yet appeared in the literature even for
n_{k}/p
bounded.
This is perhaps surprising because the ridge regression estimator is simpler to study than the Lasso or other estimators which have been studied.
The marginal characterization result we prove fills this gap.

Remark E.3

(Rates of concentration)
.

To assess the rates of concentration provided by Lemma
E.1
,
one should compare them to those which occur for the analogous fixed-design quantities.
Because proximal operators are 1-Lipschitz,

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}
are 1-Lipschitz functions of
\boldsymbol{g}_{k}^{f}
.
Because the variance
\tau_{g_{k}}^{2}\leq C/n_{k}
(see Lemma
C.1
),
Gaussian concentration of Lipschitz functions gives

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{\theta}\big(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\big)\big]\big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon\Big)\geq 1-Ce^{-cp\epsilon^{2}}.

(73)

Similarly,
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}
is Gaussian with variance
(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})\mathbf{I}_{N}
, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}
is a 1-Lipschitz function of
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}
.
Because
\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}\leq C
(by assumption
\mathsf{A1}
) and
\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}\leq Cp/n_{k}\leq C
(by assumption
\mathsf{A2}
and Lemma
C.1
),
Gaussian concentration of Lipshitz functions gives

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f,\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Big]\Big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon\Big)\geq 1-Ce^{-cp\epsilon^{2}}.

(74)

That is, in the fixed design model, those fluctuations which have probability
e^{-\Theta(p)}
have size
O(\sqrt{p/n_{k}})
.
Lemma
E.1
establishes the same for the random-design model.

Remark E.4

(High probability in
p
rather than
n_{k}
)
.

The reader may wonder why the probability bounds we provide are of the form
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
rather than
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)n_{k}}
.
One regime in which the distinction is manifest is a fixed-
p
,
n_{k}\rightarrow\infty
asymptotics.
In this limit,
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)n_{k}}\rightarrow 0
whereas
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
remains constant.
Our proof techniques are unable to establish vanishing probability bounds in this limit.
Thus, we are required to use probability bounds of the form
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
rather than
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)n_{k}}
.
We suspect, though are not certain, that this is fundamental in some cases.

Previous works (see
[
MM21
,
CMW20
]
)
typically write probability bounds in the form
\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)n_{k}}
, which decays exponentially fast in
n_{k}
.
Because these works assume an upper and lower bound on
n_{k}/p
, the two forms are in fact equivalent after adjusting constants.
The distinction becomes relevant in the current paper because—at least in the case of ridge-regression—we do not assume an upper bound on
n_{k}/p
.

E.2
Proof of marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
(i))

Here we prove Lemma
E.1
(i).
We postpone the proof of Lemma
E.1
(ii) to Section
H
.

We prove Lemma
E.1
using Gordon’s convex Gaussian min-max theorem (stated below as Lemma
E.5
) using a now standard argument
[
TOH15b
,
MM21
,
CMW20
]
.
Because
assumption
A2
does not require
n_{k}/p
be bounded above in the case of ridge-regression,
we are more explicit in tracking dependence on this quantity than current literature.

Recall Gordon’s convex min-max theorem.

Lemma E.5

(Marginal Gordon)
.

Consider any continuous function
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
which is concave in its first argument and convex in its second argument.
Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times p}
have entries
A_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{N}(0,1)
,

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{N})
, all independent,
and define

\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g},\qquad\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},

(75)

(where the subscript stands for “marginal Gordon”).

1.

If
E_{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
E_{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
are compact sets,
then for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v}+\psi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq t\Big)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}-\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle+\psi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq t\Big).

(76)

2.

If
E_{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
E_{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
are compact, convex sets,
then for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v}+\psi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq t\Big)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}-\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle+\psi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq t\Big).

(77)

To apply Gordon’s inequality to study the regression estimators marginally,
we rewrite the optimization (
36
) as a min-max problem

\min_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\big(\boldsymbol{e}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{X}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\pi})\big)-\frac{1}{2n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}+\Omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\}.

(78)

Define the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}:=-{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}
, which is distributed as in Lemma
E.5
.
If we define
\boldsymbol{v}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\pi}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})
,
the min-max problem can be written as

\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v}+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}-\frac{1}{2n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})\Big\},

(79)

where
\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\Omega_{k}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v})
.
This problem is convex-concave, and with probability 1 it has unique saddle point

\boldsymbol{v}_{k}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{k}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}=\argmax_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}).

(80)

By
[
Roc70
, Corollary 37.3.2]
, the minimization and maximization can be exchanged.

We first characterize the behavior of
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}
.
They are characterized by comparison to the fixed-design model quantities

\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{f}.

(81)

For furture reference, we point out that by Eq. (
39
)

\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{k}^{f}-\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{k}}\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\}.

(82)

Consider
1
-Lipschitz functions
\phi_{v}:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

and
\phi_{u}:({\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}})^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
.
We will show that with high probability

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\in E_{v}(\epsilon):=\Big\{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\big|\phi_{v}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})\big]\big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon\Big\},\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\in E_{u}(\epsilon):=\Big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}:\Big|\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Bigm|\{\boldsymbol{e}_{l}\}_{l}\Big]\Big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon\Big\}.\end{gathered}

(83)

Note that the set
E_{u}(\epsilon)
depends implicitly on
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
, though for compactness we supress this in the notation.
To establish the previous display, we show that the objectives in Eq. (
80
) are sub-optimal when these constraints are violated.
Precisely:

Lemma E.6

(Marginal control of primary objective)
.

There exists
\ell_{k}^{*}=\ell_{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k},n_{k},p,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\sigma_{k},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},\Omega_{k})
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\Big|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{k}^{*}\Big|=\Big|\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{k}^{*}\Big|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\\
\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon^{2},\qquad\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\epsilon)}\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon^{2}.\end{gathered}

(84)

Lemma
E.6
is proved using the marginal Gordon inequality (Lemma
E.5
), as described in the following sections.

First we prove the marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
) using Lemma
E.6
.

Proof of marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
(i)).

By the optimality of
\boldsymbol{u}_{k},\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
(Eq. (
80
)),
when Eq. (
84
) occurs we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\in E_{u}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\in E_{v}(\epsilon)
.
Moreover, by Gaussian concentration of Lipschitz functions,
with probability at least
1-Ce^{-pc\epsilon^{2}}
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Bigm|\{\boldsymbol{e}_{l}\}_{l}\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Big]\Big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon.

(85)

Combined with Lemma
E.6
,
we conclude that for
\epsilon\leq c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\displaystyle\big|\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})]\big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,\qquad\Big|\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Big]\Big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon.

(86)

We now establish the second line of Lemma
E.1
.
Restricted to the coordinates
\mathcal{I}_{k}
,
the debiased estimate of the noise can be written as (see Eq. (
37
))
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}=\zeta_{k}^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}
,
which is
C
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}
because
\zeta_{k}>c>0
by Lemma
C.1
.
Then
\phi_{e}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)
is
C
-Lipschitz in
\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}
,
so that the second line of Lemma
E.1
follows.

We establish the first line of Lemma
E.1
first for OLS, ridge regression, and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso with
\alpha_{k}>0
,
and then extend the result to the Lasso.
For simplicity of notation, we remove the subscript
k
on
\alpha_{k}
in what follows.
For OLS, ridge regression, and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso,
the penalty is differentiable, so that
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})/n_{k}=\nabla\Omega(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k})={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\nabla\bar{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})
.
Then, the estimate and
the debiased estimate of the parameter can be written as

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}:={\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{k},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}:={\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\Big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{k}}\nabla\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k})\Big),

(87)

and likewise

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}:={\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}:={\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\Big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{k}}\nabla\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})\Big),

(88)

To simplify the following exposition, we adopt the convention that if we use OLS or ridge regression we set
\alpha=1
,
and if we use the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso, then
\alpha
is the smoothing constant.
For OLS, ridge regression, and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso,
\nabla\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})/\zeta_{k}
is
C/\alpha
-Lipschitz.
Indeed,
\nabla\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\nabla\Omega_{k}({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v})
,
whence, by assumption
\mathsf{A1}
, the Lipschitz constant of
\nabla\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})
is bounded by
c
times the Lipschitz constant of
\nabla\Omega_{k}
.
For ridge-regression, this Lipschitz constant is
\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\lambda\leq C
, and for the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso it is
\lambda/\alpha\leq C/\alpha
.
Because
\zeta_{k}\geq c
,
we conclude
\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}})
is
C/\alpha
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
.
Using Eq. (
86
),
we conclude that
for
\epsilon\leq c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\displaystyle\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}})-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]\big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\frac{\epsilon}{\alpha}.

(89)

We now remove the dependence on
\alpha
in the previous display so that the upper bound does not blow up as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and so that we may extend the results to the Lasso (i.e.,
\alpha=0
).
To do so, we use the approximation technique of
[
CMW20
]
.
The main idea is to argue that that
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso estimate and debiased estimate are close to the Lasso estimate and debiased estimate for
\alpha
small (but with the regularization parameter
\lambda
kept unchanged).
Recall that for the Lasso we assume
c\leq n_{k}/p\leq C
, so that in what follows we may exchange
n_{k}
and
p
by adjusting constants.

We consider both that
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso for some
\alpha>0
and the Lasso estimators computed on the same data in the second regression.
We introduce notation which distinguishes quantities related to the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso from those related to the Lasso by adding a subscript
\alpha
to the former.
For example,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}
will denote the Lasso estimate, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}
will denote the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso estimate.

Lemma B.8 in
[
CMW20
]
gives that for
\alpha<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cp}
we have
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}\|_{2}<C\sqrt{\alpha}
.
The proof of Theorem 10 in
[
CMW20
]
(see, in particular, Eq. (80)) gives that for
\alpha<c^{\prime}

with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha)e^{-p\mathsf{c}(\alpha)}
we have
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}}\|_{2}<C\sqrt{\alpha}
.
(Note that the bound stated in
[
CMW20
]
applies to the debiased Lasso which uses
1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}/n_{k}
in place of
\zeta_{k}
. Replacing
1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}_{k}/n_{k}
by
\zeta_{k}
removes an error term, so that the bound of
[
CMW20
]
still holds).
Thus, with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha)e^{-p\mathsf{c}(\alpha)}
we have
\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{\mathrm{d}})-\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})\big|<C\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Applying the same bound with
\alpha^{\prime}
in place of
\alpha
and
0\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
,
we have with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha)e^{-p\mathsf{c}(\alpha)}
that
\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})-\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})\big|<C\sqrt{\alpha}
.
We further have the following lemma, proved in Section
M.1
.

Lemma E.7
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.
Then for
0\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha<c^{\prime}
,

\big|\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})]-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]\big|\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{\alpha}.

(90)

Combining these results,
we have
for
\alpha,\epsilon<c^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
with probability at least

1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha,\epsilon)e^{-p\mathsf{c}(\alpha\wedge\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]\big|\leq\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})-\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})\big|+\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]\big|

(91)

\displaystyle+\big|\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]-\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{f},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}^{f,\mathrm{d}})]\big|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{\alpha}+C^{\prime}\,\epsilon/\alpha+C^{\prime}\sqrt{\alpha}.

Taking
\alpha=\epsilon^{2/3}
and adjusting constants,
we conclude the first bound in Lemma
E.1
for any smoothing parameter
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\epsilon^{2/3}
.
For
\alpha^{\prime}>\epsilon^{2/3}
, the result follows by Eq. (
89
).
∎

Remark E.8
.

Existing applications of Gordon’s inequality to regression problems prove a version of Lemma
E.6
for a value
\ell_{k}^{*}
which depends on the model parameters
n_{k},p,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}
and penalty
\Omega_{k}
but not on the realization of the noise
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}

[
TOH15b
,
MM21
,
CMW20
]
.
By proving Lemma
E.6
for an
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
-dependent value
\ell_{k}^{*}
,
we can achieve control of
\boldsymbol{v}_{k},\boldsymbol{u}_{k}
which improves as
n_{k}/p\rightarrow\infty
. (See Remark
E.2
).

To localize
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
(the discussion for
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}
is analogous), Lemma
E.6
shows the minimization problem Eq. (
80
) is sub-optimal on
E_{v}(\epsilon)^{c}
.
For our proof strategy to work,
we must choose a set
E_{v}(\epsilon)^{c}
separated enough from the minimizer so that the sub-optimality on this set dominates the fluctuations of the minimal value of the optimization.
The smaller these fluctuations, the smaller we can make the separation.

Unconditionally on
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
, the fluctuations will be too large.
Indeed, consider the case of least-squares. Then, the value of the optimization problem is distributed
\chi_{n_{k}-p}^{2}/n_{k}
.
For
n_{k}/p>1+c
, this fluctuates by an amount
C^{\prime}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon^{2}
with probability at least
Ce^{-cp\epsilon^{4}}
.
Lemma
E.6
shows that conditionally on
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
,
the minimal value fluctuates by the much smaller amount
C^{\prime}(p/n_{k})\epsilon^{2}
with probability at most
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
.
By allowing
\ell_{k}^{*}
to depend on
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
,
we can localize the minimal value of the optimization problem with resolution
(p/n_{k})\epsilon^{2}
rather than
\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon^{2}
, and hence the location of the minimizer with resolution
\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon
rather than
(p/n_{k})^{1/4}\epsilon
.

See the proof of Lemma
E.6
for further details.

We prove Lemma
E.6
in the next section.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
E.6
: marginal control of primary objective

We prove Lemma
E.6
using the marginal Gordon inequality (Lemma
E.5
).
Define the auxilliary objective
