\begin{gathered}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=-\frac{1}{n_{k}}\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\langle\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}-\frac{1}{2n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}),\end{gathered}

(92)

where
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
and
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
are defined as in Lemma
E.5
.
Define

\tau_{e_{k}}^{*}=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\qquad e_{k}^{*}:=\zeta_{k}(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2},\qquad\omega_{k}:=\mathbb{E}\big[\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})\big].

(93)

We will see that
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
concentrates on
e_{k}^{*}
,

\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})
concentrates on
\omega_{k}
, and
the value of the auxilliary optimization
L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k},\boldsymbol{v}_{k})
concentrates on

\ell_{k}^{*}:=\ell_{k}^{*}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k},n_{k},p,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\sigma_{k},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},\Omega_{k}):=\frac{1}{2}e_{k}^{*2}+\omega_{k}.

(94)

(Note the dependence of
\ell_{k}^{*}
on the model parameters
n_{k},p,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k},\sigma_{k},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and penalty
\Omega_{k}
occurs via the fixed-point parameters defined via Eq. (
42
)).
We will consider the min-max problem under the compact constraints

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R,\qquad\mathcal{S}_{u}:=\Big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}\Bigm|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\big\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\big\|_{2}\leq R^{\prime},\;\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\Big\}.

(95)

For values of
R,R^{\prime}
to be chosen below.
It will suffice that
R,R^{\prime}
satisfy
R=C_{v}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
and
R^{\prime}=C_{u}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
for
C_{v}\geq C_{\mathrm{min}}
and
C_{u}\geq C_{\min}^{\prime}(C_{v})
, where
C_{\mathrm{min}}
and
C_{\mathrm{min}}^{\prime}(\cdot)
only depend on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and the regression method.
Note here that the set
\mathcal{S}_{u}
depends implicitly on
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
, but for brevity of notation we supress this dependence.

In Section
E.3.2
,
we establish for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
the lower bounds

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon^{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\end{gathered}

(96)

where the
C^{\prime}
in both bounds is the same.
In Section
E.3.3
,
we establish for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
the upper bounds

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon^{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\end{gathered}

(97)

where the
C^{\prime}
in both bounds is the same.
The preceding probabilities are taken over the randomness in
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
.

Applying the marginal Gordon inequality (Lemma
E.5
) conditionally on
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
and then averaging over
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
,
we conclude the the previous two displays hold with
L_{k}
in place of
\ell_{k}
and adjusted constants.
Because the minimization and maximization are over compact convex sets,
by
[
Roc70
, Corollary 37.3.2]
the problem

\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

(98)

has a saddle point
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}
and minimization and maximization can be exchanged.
Thus,
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
(over
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\boldsymbol{A}
)

\begin{gathered}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon^{2},\quad\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon^{2},\\
\Big|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{k}^{*}\Big|=\Big|\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{k}^{*}\Big|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2},\end{gathered}

(99)

where the
C^{\prime}
in the previous three bounds can be taken to be equal.

To relax the restriction of the optimizations to compact sets,
we show that with high-probability
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}
fall in the interior of these sets.
Take
\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u}/\sqrt{n_{k}})=\|\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
, which is 1-Lipschitz.
For
R=C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
and
C
chosen sufficiently large, we have
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}/\sqrt{n_{k}})]\leq R/2
(see bounds on fixed point parameters in Lemma
C.1
).
Then the event in the previous display (in particular, the first and third lines) for
\epsilon\leq c^{\prime}
implies
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u}
.
Next take
\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v})=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}
.
For
R=C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
and
C
chosen sufficiently large,
we have
\mathbb{E}[\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})]\leq R/2
(see again bounds on fixed point parameters in Lemma
C.1
).
Then the event in the previous display (in particular, the second and third lines) for
\Delta\leq c^{\prime}
implies
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{2}<R
.
When both
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}
is in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u}
and
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{2}<R
,
the saddle point is in the interior of the problem’s domain, in which case it remains a saddle point when we expand the domains to
\{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}:\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0\}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
.
Thus,
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}_{k}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
.
In particular, the second line of Eq. (
99
) continues to hold when we remove the compactness constraints.

It only remains to show that we can remove the compactness constraints in the first line of Eq. (
99
).
Consider the first bound.
We have

\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\max\Big\{\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}^{c}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\Big\}.

(100)

Eq. (
99
) implies the first quantity in the maximum is smaller than
\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}(p/n_{k})\epsilon^{2}
with conditional probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
.
To bound the second quantity in the maximum, we apply Eq. (
99
) with a different choice of
\phi_{u}
; namely,
\phi_{u}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}/\sqrt{n_{k}})=\|\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
.
Taking
R^{\prime}=C^{\prime}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
in Eq. (
95
) sufficiently large and
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
sufficiently small,
we have that
E_{u}^{\prime}(\epsilon)
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u}
(where
E_{u}^{\prime}
is defined with respect to function
\phi_{u}^{\prime}
).
When Eq. (
99
) occurs with
\phi_{u}^{\prime},E_{u}^{\prime}
in place of
\phi_{u},E_{u}
,
then the saddle point
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}
satisfies
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in E_{u}^{\prime}(\epsilon)
.
Consider now any
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in S_{u}^{c}
.
Because the function
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
is concave, it is non-decreasing on the line segment joining
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}
to
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}
, and because
E_{u}^{\prime}(\epsilon)
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in E_{u}^{\prime}(\epsilon)
,
this line segment intersects with
E_{u}^{\prime c}(\epsilon)\cap\mathcal{S}_{u}
at some point
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime\prime}
.
Then,

\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{\prime c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}L_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon^{2}.

(101)

Because this applies to all
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in S_{u}^{c}
, we conclude that with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
that the second term in the maximum in Eq. (
100
) is less than
\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}(p/n_{k})\epsilon^{2}
.
Then, the left-hand side of Eq. (
100
) is also smaller than
\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}(p/n_{k})\epsilon^{2}
.
Removing the constraint
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
on the inner minimization only decreases the value of the min-max problem.
We conclude the upper bound shown in the second line of Eq. (
84
).
A similar argument allows us to remove the compactness constraints in the lower bound in the first line of Eq. (
99
).

Provided we can prove Eqs. (
96
) and (
97
),
we have concluded Lemma
E.6
.
We establish these bounds in the next three sections.

E.3.1
The good marginal characterization event

To prove Eqs. (
96
) and (
97
),
we show that the upper and lower bounds on the min-max problem are implied by the occurence of a certain high-probability event.
To describe this event, it is useful to couple the auxilliary objective (
92
) to the fixed-design models (
38
).
The coupling is given by taking

\boldsymbol{g}_{k}^{f}=\tau_{g_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{h}_{k}^{f}=\tau_{h_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}.

(102)

We introduce notation
\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{r})\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{r}
for the positive semi-definite matrix whose
(i,j)
entry is
\langle\boldsymbol{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\rangle
, where
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
for all
i
and some fixed
s
.
With some abuse of notation,
we will use the same notation
\boldsymbol{T}
regardless of the number
r
of arguments to the function and regardless of the dimensionality
s
of the vectors
\boldsymbol{a}_{k}
.
No confusion should result.

The good marginal characterization event is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\Delta):=\Big\{

\displaystyle\Big\|\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Bigm|\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\Big]\Big\|_{\mathsf{F}}<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,\;

(103)

\displaystyle\Big\|\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{k}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{k}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{\mathsf{F}}<\epsilon,\;

\displaystyle\big|\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})-\omega_{k}\big|<\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon,\;|\tau_{e_{k}}^{*}-\tau_{e_{k}}|=\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}-\tau_{e_{k}}\Big|<\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\epsilon,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\in E_{v}(\Delta/2),\;\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\in E_{u}(\Delta/2)\;\;\Big\}.

The expectations are taken in the model which couples the auxilliary objective with the fixed-design models.
In all cases, we have normalized the arguments to
\boldsymbol{T}
so that their
\ell_{2}
norm is of order 1 (see Lemma
C.1
).
Because the matrices involved are finite-dimensional, the Frobenius norm can be replaced with any norm at the cost of constant factors.
For future reference, we have written the expectations appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{k}
in Section
M.5
.
In words,
the good marginal characterization event is the event that several important quantities are close to their expectations.
It has high-probability.

Lemma E.9

(Good marginal characterization event)
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.
There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
for all
\epsilon,\Delta<c^{\prime}
the event
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\Delta)
occurs with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon,\Delta)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon\wedge\Delta)p}
.

We prove Lemma
E.9
in Section
G
.

E.3.2
Lower bounds on the auxilliary min-max problem

In this section, we prove Eq. (
96
).
In particular, we seek a lower bound on

\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),

(104)

and also on the problem where we further restrict the minimization to
\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\epsilon)
.
It is enough to show that our lower bound holds on the event
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\Delta)
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
,
\Delta=\Delta(\epsilon)<c^{\prime}
, and
c^{\prime}
taken sufficiently small and depending only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and the regression method.
We will henceforth assume we are on this event for
\epsilon,\Delta
for
c^{\prime}
sufficiently small, without repeatedly reminding the reader of this fact,
and all statements will be deterministic.
In particular,
as we derive more implications of
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\Delta)
,
we may need to take
c^{\prime}
smaller,
but will not track
c^{\prime}
or announce when an additional implication requires we shrink
c^{\prime}
further.
To make notation more compact,
we denote

\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{N},k}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)\Bigm|\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\Big],\qquad\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{P},k}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{k}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\Big)\Big].

(105)

We remind the reader that explicit expressions for these can be found in Section
M.5
.

The major steps in the analysis are as follows.

1.

We replace
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
by a function
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
which approximates it uniformly well across its domain.
We control the change in the value of the min-max problem incurred by this replacement.
The objective
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
is introduced because it is easier to analyze.

2.

Ideally, we would evaluate the internal maximization exactly.
However, doing so explicitly leads to complicated expressions.
Instead, we construct a lower bound on the internal maximization.
In particular, for any function
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})
for which
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{S}_{u}
, the quantity
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v})
is a lower bound on the value of the maximization problem.
Our strategy is to pick a function
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})
so that the function
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v})
is tractable to analyze and provides a good enough lower bound for our purposes.

3.

We establish several properties of the lower bound
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
.
In particular, we show
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})
is close to
\ell_{k}^{*}
,
the subdifferential

\partial\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})
contains a small element, and
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
is strongly convex for bounded
\boldsymbol{v}
.

4.

Using standard convex analysis techniques,
these properties imply a lower bound on the min-max problem over
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
and over
\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\epsilon)\cap\{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\}
.

We now carry out these steps in detail.

Step 1: we replace
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
by a function
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
which approximates it uniformly well across its domain.

We use a Gram-Schmidt type procedure to replace
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
.
In particular,
we replace
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
by
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}^{\mathrm{gs}}

so that

\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}/\sqrt{n_{k}},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}/\sqrt{n_{k}})=\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{N},k}.

(106)

By Eq. (
103
),
we may do this in such a way that
\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}\leq C^{\prime}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon
.
The only term in
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
which depends on
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
is
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\rangle/n_{k}
.
For
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R,\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}
,
we bound

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\rangle}{n_{k}}-\frac{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}},\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\rangle}{n_{k}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}-\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}}{n_{k}}\leq C\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon,

(107)

where we have used that
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R=C_{v}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
(see Eq. (
95
)) and

\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}+C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
.
We define
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
like
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
, except with
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}^{\mathrm{gs}}
in place of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
.
Thus,

\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}|\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})|\leq\frac{p}{n_{k}}\epsilon.

(108)

Step 2: we construct a lower bound
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
on the internal maximization.

In particular,
we evaluate
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

at
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})
defined by
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{v}):=e_{k}^{*}\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}}{(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}(\boldsymbol{v})=0
.
We have chosen
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})
so that
\|\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}=e_{k}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{v})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}})/n_{k}=e_{k}^{*}(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
(see Eqs. (
106
) and (
320
)).
Evaluating
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
at this value of
\boldsymbol{u}
gives

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{v})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}})-\frac{1}{n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})\|_{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle-\frac{1}{2{n_{k}}}\|\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})

(109)

\displaystyle=e_{k}^{*}\Big(\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}e_{k}^{*2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}).

Note

\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\leq\zeta_{k}\Big|\sqrt{\frac{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}}{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}}-1\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}+\frac{e_{k}^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}{(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}},

(110)

where we have used that
\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}\leq Cp/n_{k}
,
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{v}^{2}p/n_{k}
,
\zeta_{k}\leq 1
,
\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}\geq c
,
e_{k}^{*}\leq C
, and
\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{gs}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}=1
(see Lemma
C.1
, Eq. (
95
), Eq. (
106
), and Eq. (
320
)).
Thus, we may choose
R^{\prime}=C_{u}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
in Eq. (
95
) large enough so that

\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathcal{S}_{u}
and
C_{u}
depends only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, the regression method, and
C_{v}
.
Then

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}).

(111)

Step 3: we establish several properties of the lower bound
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
.

First, we show
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})
is close to
\ell_{k}^{*}
.
We compute

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})=e_{k}^{*}\Big(\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}e_{k}^{*2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}).

(112)

We approximate several quantities in this expression.
In particular, we make replacements

\begin{gathered}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\longrightarrow\tau_{h_{k}}^{2},\qquad\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\longrightarrow\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}},\qquad\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})\longrightarrow\omega_{k},\end{gathered}

(113)

after which, by simple algebra, we get the quantity
\ell_{k}^{*}
(see its definition in Eq. (
94
)).

By Eqs. (
162
) and (
320
),
the terms above differ from their replacements by at must
(p/n_{k})\epsilon
.
Indeed, for the first replacement, we use that
(n_{k}/p)\big|\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}-\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}\big|<\epsilon
.
For the second replacement,
we use that
\big|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}/\sqrt{n_{k}}-\tau_{g_{k}}\mathsf{df}_{k}/\sqrt{n_{k}}\big|<(p/n_{k})\epsilon
,

\tau_{g_{k}}=(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
(by Eq. (
57
)),
and
(\mathsf{df}_{k}/n_{k})\big|(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2}-(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2}\big|\leq(p/n_{k})^{3/2}\epsilon
(recall
\mathsf{df}_{k}\leq p
).
The error incurred by the third replacement is directly controlled by Eq. (
162
).
On the event
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon)
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
, we have
e_{k}^{*}\leq C
.
Thus,

\Big|\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})-\ell_{k}^{*}\Big|<C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon.

(114)

Second, we show
\partial\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})
contains a small element.
The subdifferential at
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}
is

\displaystyle\partial\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})

\displaystyle=e_{k}^{*}\frac{\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}}{\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}}-\frac{e_{k}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}+\partial\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}).

(115)

If we replace
\tau_{e_{k}}^{*}
by
\tau_{e_{k}}
,
e_{k}^{*}
by
\sqrt{n_{k}}\,\tau_{g_{k}}\zeta_{k}
, and
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}
by
\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}
(we bound the error incurred by these replacements below) and recall that
(\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2}=\sqrt{n_{k}}\,\tau_{g_{k}}
(see Eq. (
57
)),
we get

\zeta_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}-\tau_{g_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g})+\partial\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}).

(116)

By the definition of
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}
in Eqs. (
39
) and (
81
),

\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\big\{\|\tau_{g_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}/2+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})/\zeta_{k}\big\}
.
By the KKT conditions for this optimization, the set in the previous display contains
\boldsymbol{0}
.

We control the errors introduced by these replacements.
By Eqs. (
93
), (
162
), (
320
), (
57
),
and the bound
\tau_{h_{k}}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
(see Lemma
C.1
),
each term we replace is within
\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon
of its replacement.
Moreover, because
e_{k}^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
,
\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}\geq c
, and
\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
,
the derivatives with respect to the terms we replace are bounded by
C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
.
We conclude

\inf\big\{\|\boldsymbol{\delta}\|_{2}:\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\partial\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})\big\}<C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon

(117)

Third, we show that
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
is strongly convex for bounded
\boldsymbol{v}
.
Because
\bar{\Omega}_{k}
is convex and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}
is linear,
it suffices to lower bound the Hessian of
e_{k}^{*}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}
.
The Hessian is

\frac{e_{k}^{*}}{\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}}{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\Big)\succeq\frac{e_{k}^{*}\tau_{e_{k}}^{2}}{(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\,\mathbf{I}_{p}.

(118)

By Lemma
C.1
, Eq. (
162
), and because
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
for
R=C_{v}\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\leq C
,
the coefficient in the previous display is no smaller than
c>0
.
Thus,

\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
is
c
-strongly convex on
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
.

Step 4: we use these properties to establish lower bounds on the min-max problem.

Because
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})
is
c
-strongly convex on
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
,
for any
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\partial\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\geq\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})+\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})+\frac{c}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}

(119)

\displaystyle\geq\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f})-\frac{16}{c}\|\boldsymbol{\delta}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{c}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining the previous display with
Eqs. (
108
), (
111
), (
114
), and (
117
),
we conclude

\begin{gathered}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\frac{c}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2},\\
\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{k}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon.\end{gathered}

(120)

Further, by Eq. (
162
),

\big|\phi_{v}\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\big)-\phi_{v}^{*}\big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\frac{\Delta}{2}.

(121)

By the definition of
E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)
and because
\phi_{v}
is
1
-Lipschitz,

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R\end{subarray}}\;\frac{c}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}\geq c\,\frac{p}{n_{k}}\frac{\Delta^{2}}{16}.

(122)

Combining Eq. (
120
) with the previous display and taking
\Delta=\sqrt{32C^{\prime}\epsilon/c}
(with the same values
c,C^{\prime}
appearing in Eq. (
120
) and the previous display),
we conclude the lower bound inside the first probability in Eq. (
96
) holds.
The lower bound inside the second probability in Eq. (
96
) is given in Eq. (
120
).

In summary, we have shown that the lower bounds inside the probabilities in Eq. (
96
) hold on
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\sqrt{32C^{\prime}\epsilon/c})
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
E.9
, we conclude that the probability bounds in Eq. (
96
) hold as well.

E.3.3
Upper bounds on the auxilliary max-min problem

In this section, we prove Eq. (
97
).
In particular, we seek a upper bound on

\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\;\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),

(123)

and also on the problem where we further restrict the minimization to
\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\epsilon)
.
As in the previous section, we will show that our upper bound holds on the event
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\Delta)
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
,
\Delta=\Delta(\epsilon)<c^{\prime}
, and
c^{\prime}
taken sufficiently small and depending only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and the regression method.
We will henceforce assume we are on this event for
\epsilon,\Delta
for
c^{\prime}
sufficiently small, without repeatedly reminding the reader of this fact,
and all statements will be deterministic.

The major steps in the analysis are as in the previous section, except that the first and second steps occur in the opposite order.
We now carry out these steps in detail.

Step 1: we construct an upper bound on the internal minimization.

In particular,
we evaluate
\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
at
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}
.
Define

\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\frac{1}{n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}+\frac{1}{n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\frac{1}{2{n_{k}}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{v}).

(124)

Then

\max_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}}\;\;\;\;\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u}).

(125)

Indeed, by taking
R=C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
with
C
sufficiently large,
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}\leq R
by
Eqs. (
162
), (
320
), and Lemma
C.1
.
The previous display follows.

Step 2: we replace
\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u})
by a function
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})
which approximates it uniformly well across its domain.

In particular, we make the replacements given in Eq. (
113
),
after which we get the objective

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\tau_{h_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}})-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}}\,\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}}+\omega_{k}.

(126)

As argued after Eq. (
113
),
the terms in Eq. (
113
) differ from their replacements by at most
(p/n_{k})\epsilon
.
Using the
\delta
-method,
we can conclude

\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}<2e_{k}^{*}}|\ell_{k}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{u})-\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})|<C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon,

(127)

provided the coefficients of the terms we replace are appropriately bounded with high-probability.
Indeed, the coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
is
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
, which is bounded by
C
when
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}
(we have used Eq. (
162
) and assumption
A2
).
Because
\tau_{h_{k}}\geq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
(see Lemma
C.1
),
by the
\delta
-method we have
\big|\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}\|_{2}-\tau_{h_{k}}\big|<\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\epsilon
, and the coefficient of
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}
is
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}/n_{k}\leq\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}/n_{k}+R\|\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\,\epsilon
by Eq. (
162
), (
320
), and Lemma
C.1
.
Thus, the replacement of
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}
by
\tau_{h_{k}}
incurs an error bounded by
C(p/n_{k})\epsilon
.
Finally,
the coefficient of
\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v})
is 1.

Step 3: we establish several properties of the upper bound
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})
.

First, we approximate the value
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})
.
Recalling that
\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}=\zeta_{k}(\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\tau_{h_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}})
,
we compute

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}\zeta_{k}}-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}}\,\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}{n_{k}}+\omega_{k}.

(128)

We replace the quantity
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
with
e_{k}^{*}
.
If we make this replacement, we get
\ell_{k}^{*}
(recall the definition of
\ell_{k}^{*}
, Eq. (
94
)).
Using
\tau_{h_{k}}\leq C\sqrt{p/n_{k}}
(see Lemma
C.1
), Eq. (
162
), and Eq. (
320
),
we conclude that the term
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
differs from
e_{k}^{*}
by at most
(p/n_{k})\epsilon
.
By the
\delta
-method,

\big|\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})-\ell_{k}^{*}\big|<C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon,

(129)

provided we can show the derivatives with respect to the terms we replace are appropriately bounded.
Indeed, they are because
\zeta_{k}\geq c
and
\mathsf{df}_{k}/n_{k}\leq 1
(see Lemma
C.1
).

Second, we show the gradient of
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})
at
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}
is small.
We only compute the gradient for the coordinates in
\mathcal{I}_{k}
.
Its norm is

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\big\|_{2}=\Big\|\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}+\tau_{h_{k}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{n_{k}}-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}}-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{n_{k}}\Big\|_{2}

(130)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big(\zeta_{k}^{-1}-\frac{\mathsf{df}_{k}}{n_{k}}\sqrt{\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}}-1\Big)\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}

We replace the quantity
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{k}}
with
e_{k}^{*}=\zeta_{k}(\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2})^{1/2}
.
As argued above, it differs from its replacement by at most
(p/n_{k})\epsilon
.
After this replacement, the right-hand side becomes 0.
By the
\delta
-method,

\big\|\nabla\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\big\|_{2}<C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}^{3/2}}\,\epsilon,

(131)

provided we can show show the derivatives with respect to the terms we replace are appropriately bounded.
Indeed, they are because
\tau_{e_{k}}^{*2}+\tau_{h_{k}}^{2}\leq C
and
e_{k}^{*}\geq c>0
(see Eq. (
162
) and Lemma
C.1
) and
\mathsf{df}_{k}/n_{k}\leq 1
.

Third, observe that by Eq. (
126
),

\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})
is
1/n_{k}
-strongly concave everywhere.

Step 4: we use these properties to establish a upper bounds on the max-min problem.

Because
\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})
is
1/n_{k}
-strongly concave,
for any
\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c}}=0
,

\displaystyle\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})+\nabla\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}-\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f})-\frac{1}{2n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}

(132)

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})+n_{k}\cdot\|\nabla\ell_{k}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f})_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{4n_{k}}\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining the previous display with Eqs. (
125
), (
127
), (
129
), and (
132
),
we conclude

\begin{gathered}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon-\;\;\;\min_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}}\;\;\;\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}{4n_{k}},\\
\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq R}\ell_{k}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{k}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\epsilon.\end{gathered}

(133)

Further,
by Lemma
E.9
, after adjusting constants and using the
\sqrt{p/n_{k}}\leq C
,

\Big|\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big)-\phi_{u}^{*}\Big|\leq\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{k}}}\,\frac{\Delta}{2}.

(134)

By the definition of
E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)
and because
\phi_{u}
is
1
-Lipschitz

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}^{c}(\Delta)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}_{k}^{f}\|_{2}^{2}}{4n_{k}}\geq\frac{p}{n_{k}}\,\frac{\Delta^{2}}{16}.

(135)

Combining Eq. (
133
) with the previous display and taking
\Delta=\sqrt{32C^{\prime}\epsilon}
(with the same value
C^{\prime}
appearing in Eq. (
133
)),
we conclude the upper bound inside the probability in the first line of Eq. (
97
) holds.
The upper bound inside the probability in the second line of Eq. (
97
) is given in Eq. (
133
).

In summary, we have shown that the lower bounds inside the probabilities in Eq. (
97
) hold on
\mathcal{G}_{k}(\epsilon,\sqrt{32C^{\prime}\epsilon})
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
E.9
, we conclude that the probability bounds in Eq. (
97
) hold as well.

Appendix F
Conditional characterization

In this section, we prove the conditional characterization (Lemma
D.3
).

F.1
The conditional Gordon inequality for regression

To prove Lemma
D.3
, we use a Gordon-like comparison inequality,
which we call the
conditional Gordon inequality for regression
, stated below in Lemma
F.2
.
Like the marginal Gordon inequality (Lemma
E.5
),
the conditional Gordon inequality involves comparing the Gaussian process
L_{2}
in
Eq. (
79
) to a simpler stochastic process.
Unlike in the marginal Gordon inequality, the simpler process is not Gaussian.
It is defined using Gaussian vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{N})
,
but also using non-Gaussian vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
which are functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
.
In particular, the simpler process is defined on the same probability space as the random design models.

We first define
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}
.
We warn the reader that the definition is complicated and non-intuitive, and is carefully chosen to make the comparison inequality hold.
Some intuition for these definitions is given in Section
L
, where the comparison inequality is proved.
Define
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}

by

\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}),\quad\boldsymbol{s}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}^{\top}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{1}}-{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}),\quad\boldsymbol{t}_{1}={\boldsymbol{X}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}).

(136)

Note that
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{1}
are functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
.
Given
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{1})
,
we define vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
as the unique solution to the equations

\displaystyle-\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}}\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\rangle+\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\|_{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}-\boldsymbol{t}_{1}=0,\qquad-\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}+\frac{\boldsymbol{v}_{1}}{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\|_{2}}\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h},\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\rangle+\boldsymbol{s}_{1}=0,

(137)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h},\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\rangle}{n_{1}}=\zeta_{1}\tau_{h_{1}}.

(138)

These equations indeed have a unique solution:

Lemma F.1
.

For
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
defined by Eq. (
136
),
where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}
is defined by Eq. (
36
),
the equations (
137
) and (
138
) have a unique solution.

Lemma
F.1
is proved in Section
L
.
We are ready to state the conditional Gordon inequality for regression.

Lemma F.2

(Conditional Gordon inequality for regression)
.

Define
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}
to be the unique solution to Eqs. (
137
) and (
138
),
where
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{s}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{1}
are defined by Eq. (
136
),
and let
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{N})
independent of each other and everything else.
Define

\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\frac{\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}+\|\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{v}_{1}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g},\qquad\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\frac{\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle}{\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\|_{2}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}+\|\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{v}_{1}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{u}_{1}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h},

(139)

(where the subscript stands for “conditional Gordon”),
and define the conditional auxilliary objective

\displaystyle\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}-\frac{1}{2{n_{2}}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{2}(\boldsymbol{v}).

(140)

1.

If
E_{u}=E_{u}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
E_{v}=E_{v}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
are (possibly
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
-dependent) compact sets,
then for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq t\right)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq t\right).

(141)

2.

If
E_{u}=E_{u}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
E_{v}=E_{v}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
are (possibly
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
-dependent) compact, convex sets,
then for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq t\right)\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq t\right).

(142)

Lemma
F.2
is a consequence of a more general comparison inequality which may be of independent interest.
Both Lemma
F.2
and the more general comparison inequality are proved in Section
L
.

Remark F.3
.

The conditional Gordon inequality for regression takes the same form as the marginal Gordon inequality in Section
E.2
,
except that
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
,
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
are replaced by
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
,
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
.
The quantity
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
has a Gaussian distribution with variance
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
, and the quantity
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
has a Gaussian distribution with variance
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{N}
.
This is not true of the quantities
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
and
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
,
but we encourage the reader to adopt the intuition that it is, in a certain sense, approximately true.
Indeed,
we will show using the marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
) that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}
behave like standard Gaussian vectors (where “behave like” will be in the sense of concentration of Lipschitz functions, as in the marginal characterization).
Motivated by this intuition,
if we replace
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}
with a standard Gaussian vector and replace
\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{v}_{1}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}
with
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}
(which, we will see, negligibly impacts the min-max problem),
then
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{cg}}(\boldsymbol{u})
has a Gaussian distribution with variance
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
and correlation
\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle/(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2})
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}
.
Similarly,
\boldsymbol{h}_{\mathrm{mg}}(\boldsymbol{v})
behaves like a Gaussian distribution with variance
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{p}
and correlation
\langle\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle/(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2})
with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}
.
Thus, we should think of the conditional Gordon inequality as having the same structure as the marginal Gordon inequality, except that the Gaussian vectors are correlated with the quantities
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}
coming from the first regression, with the correlation is determined implicitly by
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
, respectively.

F.2
Proof of the conditional characterization (Lemma
D.3
)

We now define
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})
which appear
in the statement of Lemma
D.3
:

\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1}):=\tau_{g_{1}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1}):=\tau_{h_{1}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h},\quad\text{for $\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}$ satisfying Eqs.~\eqref{eq:hat-xi} and \eqref{eq:constraint+}.}

(143)

As in the proof of the marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
),
we first characterize the behavior of
\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
by comparison to the fixed-design model quantities
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{f}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{f}
.
Consider functions
\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v};\mathsf{Cond}_{1})
which is 1-Lipschitz in its first argument
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u};\mathsf{Cond}_{1})
which is 1-Lipschitz in its first argument
\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}
.
For simplicity of notation, we will often write
\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v})
and
\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u})
, but the dependence of
\phi_{v}
and
\phi_{u}
on
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
will be understood.
We show that with high probability

\begin{gathered}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in E_{v|1}(\epsilon):=\Big\{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\Big|\phi_{v}\big(\boldsymbol{v}\big)-\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{f})\bigm|\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\big]\Big|<\epsilon\Big\},\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon):=\Big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{k}}:\Big|\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi_{u}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big)\Bigm|\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},\,\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\Big]\Big|<\epsilon\Big\}.\end{gathered}

(144)

Analogous to our proof of the marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
),
we will show this by proving the objectives in Eq. (
80
) are sub-optimal when these constraints are violated.
Precisely:

Lemma F.4

(Conditional control of primary objective)
.

There exists a constant
\ell_{2|1}^{*}=\ell_{2|1}^{*}(\tau_{e_{2}},n_{2},p,{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2},\Omega_{2})
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\Big|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{2|1}^{*}\Big|=\Big|\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{2|1}^{*}\Big|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2},\\
\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2},\qquad\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v|1}^{c}(\epsilon)}\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0\end{subarray}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{2|1}^{*}+C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}.\end{gathered}

(145)

Lemma
F.4
is proved using the conditional Gordon inequality (Lemma
F.2
), as described in the following sections.

First we prove the conditional characterization (Lemma
D.3
) using Lemma
F.4
.

Proof of conditional characterization (Lemma
D.3
).

By the optimality of
\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
(Eq. (
80
)),
when Eq. (
145
) occurs we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in E_{v|1}(\epsilon)
.
We conclude that for
\epsilon\leq c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in E_{v|1}(\epsilon)
.

We now establish the second line of Lemma
D.3
.
Restricted to the coordinates
\mathcal{I}_{2}
,
the debiased estimate of the noise can be written as (see Eq. (
37
))
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}=\zeta_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}
,
which is
C
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}
by Lemma
C.1
.
Further, the quantities
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}},\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}
are functions of
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
(see Eqs. (
37
) and (
65
)).
Thus,
\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\Big\}\Big)
is a function of
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
and
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
and is
M_{2}
-Lipschitz in the first two.
It is thus a function of
\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
and
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
and is
CM_{2}
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
.
Taking
\phi_{u}
to be
1/M_{2}
times this function and adjusting the
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and regression-method dependent constants,
the event in the second line of Lemma
D.3
is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\not\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
.
The probability bound in the second line of Lemma
D.3
follows by the above discussion.

We now establish the first line of Lemma
D.3
for OLS, ridge regression, and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso with
\alpha_{k}>0
, and then extend the result to the Lasso.
To simplify notation, we remove the subscript
2
from
\alpha_{2}
.
As in the proof of Lemma
E.1
, we adopt the convention that if we use OLS or ridge regression we set
\alpha=1
, and if we use the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso, then
\alpha
is the smoothing constant.
For OLS, ridge regression, and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso,
we recall the representation of the estimate and debiased estimate from Eq. (
87
) in the proof of Lemma
E.1
.
As argued there,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}
is
C/\alpha
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
.
Then
\phi_{\theta}(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\})
is a function of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}^{\mathrm{d}}
, and
\mathsf{Cond}_{1}
,
and is
M_{2}/\alpha
-Lipschitz in the first two.
It it is thus
CM_{2}/\alpha
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
.
Thus, for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
, with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
,

\big|\phi_{\theta}(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\})-\phi_{\theta|1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})\big|\leq M_{2}\epsilon/\alpha.

(146)

We now remove the dependence on
\alpha
in the previous display so that the upper bound does not blow up as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
and so that we may extend the results to the Lasso (i.e.,
\alpha=0
).
To do so, we use the approximation technique of
[
CMW20
]
and in the proof of Lemma
E.1
.
As we did there, we consider using the Lasso and the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso in the second regression.
We introduce notation which distinguishes quantities related to the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso from those related to the Lasso by adding a subscript
\alpha
to the former.
For example,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}
will denote the Lasso estimate, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha}
will denote the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso estimate.
Each choice of regression method for the second regression leads to a distinct fixed design model.
We define
\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha)}
to be the function defined by Eq. (
67
) in the fixed design model corresponding to the use of the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso in the second regression.

As argued in the proof of Lemma
E.1
,
for
0\leq\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha<c
,
we have with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\alpha)p}
that
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha}\|_{2}<C^{\prime}\sqrt{\alpha}
and
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}}\|_{2}<C^{\prime}\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Moreover,
we have the following lemma,
proved in Section
M.1
.

Lemma F.5
.

Under
A1
and
A2
,
we have for
0\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\alpha)p}
that

\big|\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha^{\prime})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})-\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\big|<C\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{2}}}\;\Big)\sqrt{\alpha}.

(147)

Combining these bounds,
we have for
\alpha,\epsilon<c^{\prime}
and
0\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\alpha,\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\alpha,\epsilon)p}
that

\displaystyle\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})

\displaystyle-\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha^{\prime})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\big|\leq\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{d}})-\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})\big|

(148)

\displaystyle+\big|\phi_{\theta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2,\alpha}^{\mathrm{d}})-\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\big|+\big|\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})-\phi_{\theta|1}^{(\alpha^{\prime})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}M_{2}\sqrt{\alpha}+M_{2}\epsilon/\alpha+C\Big(M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{1}}}+M_{2}\sqrt{\frac{p}{n_{2}}}\;\Big)\sqrt{\alpha}.

If we take
\alpha=\epsilon^{2/3}

and
adjust
\mathsf{c}(\epsilon),\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)
,
we get, in the case of the
\alpha
-smoothed Lasso with smoothing parameter
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\epsilon^{2/3}
,
the upper bound provided by the first line in Lemma
D.3
.
For
\alpha^{\prime}>\epsilon^{2/3}
, we may directly apply Eq. (
89
) to get the first line in Lemma
D.3
.
∎

Remark F.6

(Rates of concentration)
.

The rate of concentration provided by Lemma
D.3
is slower than that provided by the marginal characterization (Lemma
E.1
),
and is slower than the corresponding rate of concentration in the fixed design models (see Remark
E.3
).
In the fixed design models,
\boldsymbol{g}_{2}^{f}
and
\boldsymbol{h}_{2}^{f}
have a Gaussian distribution conditional on
\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1}),\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}(\mathsf{Cond}_{1})
,
with variance
\tau_{g_{2}}^{2}\rho_{g}^{\perp^{2}}
and
\tau_{h_{2}}^{2}\rho_{h}^{\perp 2}
, respectively.
By Lemma
C.1
,
these variances are of order
1/n_{2}
and
p/n_{2}
.
Thus, in the fixed design models, with conditional probability at least
1-Ce^{-cp\epsilon^{2}}

the quantities
\phi_{\theta}(\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f}\},\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{f,\mathrm{d}}\})
and
\phi_{e}\Big(\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\},\Big\{\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{\mathrm{d}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big\}\Big)
fluctuate by no more than
C^{\prime}M_{2}\sqrt{p/n_{2}}\,\epsilon
from their conditional mean.
The bound provided by Lemma
D.3
is worse by a factor
\sqrt{n_{2}/p}
, which can be large under assumption
\mathsf{A2}
.
We do not know whether this discrepancy is fundamental or an artefact of our proofs.

We prove Lemma
F.4
in the next section.

F.3
Proof of Lemma
F.4
: conditional control of the primary objective

It is useful to introduce a new fixed-design model which we call
conditional fixed-design model
.
The conditional fixed-design model is defined on the same probability space as the conditional auxilliary objective (
140
).
It is

\begin{gathered}\textbf{Conditional fixed-design model}\\
\textbf{for parameter estimation}\\
\boldsymbol{Y}^{cf}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}+\boldsymbol{G}^{cf}\;\;\text{(CFD-P)}\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}\textbf{Conditional Fixed-design model}\\
\textbf{for noise estimation}\\
\boldsymbol{R}^{cf}=\boldsymbol{E}+\boldsymbol{H}^{cf}\;\;\text{(CFD-N)}\end{gathered}

(149)

where

\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{cf}=\tau_{g_{1}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},\quad\boldsymbol{g}_{2}^{cf}=\tau_{g_{2}}(\rho_{g}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}+\rho_{g}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}),\quad\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{cf}=\tau_{h_{1}}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1},\quad\boldsymbol{h}_{2}^{cf}=\tau_{h_{2}}(\rho_{h}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h}+\rho_{h}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}).

(150)

We define parameter and noise estimates in the conditional fixed-design model just as we did in the (unconditional) fixed-design model.
In particular,

\begin{gathered}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{cf}:=\eta_{k}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{cf};\zeta_{k}):=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{cf}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{\zeta_{k}}\Omega_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\\
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{cf}:=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\Big\{\frac{\zeta_{k}}{2}\|\boldsymbol{g}_{k}^{cf}-\boldsymbol{\pi}\|_{2}^{2}+\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi})\Big\},\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{{cf},\mathrm{d}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{cf}+{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{cf}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{k}^{cf})={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{y}}_{k}^{cf}.\end{gathered}

(151)

and

\boldsymbol{u}_{k,\mathcal{I}}^{cf}=\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}^{cf}_{k,\mathcal{I}}=\begin{cases}\zeta_{k}\boldsymbol{r}^{cf}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k},\\
\boldsymbol{0}\quad&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k}^{c},\end{cases}\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{cf,\mathrm{d}}=\frac{1-\zeta_{k}}{\zeta_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{cf}+\Big(\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{cf}-\frac{1-\zeta_{k}}{\zeta_{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{k}^{cf}\Big)=\boldsymbol{r}_{k}^{cf}.

(152)

The value of the primary min-max problem concentrates on

\begin{gathered}\ell_{2|1}^{*}:=\frac{1}{2}n_{2}\tau_{g_{2}}^{2}\zeta_{2}^{2}+\omega_{2},\end{gathered}

(153)

where
\omega_{2}
is defined in Eq. (
93
).
We will consider the min-max problem under the compact constraints

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v},\qquad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}:=\Big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\Bigm|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\big\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\big\|_{2}\leq C_{u},\;\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0\Big\}.

(154)

For values of
C_{v},C_{u}
to be chosen below.
It will suffice that
C_{v},C_{u}\geq C_{\mathrm{min}}
, where
C_{\mathrm{min}}
only depends on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and the regression method.

In Section
F.3.2
,
we establish for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
the lower bounds

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{2|1}^{*}+C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\end{gathered}

(155)

where the
C^{\prime}
in both bounds is the same.
In Section
F.3.3
,
we establish for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
the upper bounds

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\\
\mathbb{P}\Big(\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}+C^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\Big)\geq 1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p},\end{gathered}

(156)

where the
C^{\prime}
in both bounds is the same.
The preceding probabilities are taken over the randomness in
\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g},\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}
.

Applying the conditional Gordon inequality for regression (Lemma
F.2
),
we conclude that the previous two displays hold with
L_{2}
in place of
\ell_{2|1}
and adjusted constants.
Because the minimization and maximization are over compact convex sets,
by
[
Roc70
, Corollary 37.3.2]
the problem

\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

(157)

has a saddle-point
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}},\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}
and minimization and maximization can be exchanged.
Thus,
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\begin{gathered}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2},\quad\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}\end{subarray}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{2|1}^{*}+C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2},\\
\Big|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{2|1}^{*}\Big|=\Big|\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})-\ell_{2|1}^{*}\Big|\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2},\end{gathered}

(158)

where the
C^{\prime}
in the previous three bounds can be taken to be equal.

To relax the restriction of the optimizations to compact sets,
we use Lemma
E.6
.
In particular, applying Lemma
E.6
for the functions
\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v})=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}
and
\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u})=\|\boldsymbol{u}-\zeta_{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
,
we conclude that the saddle point
\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
for the unconstrained problem satisfies with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
that
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{2}<C\sqrt{p/n_{2}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}-\zeta_{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}<C\sqrt{p/n_{2}}
for sufficiently large
C
.
Further, with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
, we have
\|\zeta_{2}\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}<C
, whence
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}<C
.
Thus, taking
C_{v},C_{u}
sufficiently large, we have that with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
we have
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{2}<C_{v}
and
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}<C_{u}
.
On this event,
\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
is also the saddle point for the min-max problem on the restricted domains
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\|_{2}<C_{v}
and
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}
.
That is,
\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
. Then, the value of the min-max problems in the second line of Eq. (
158
) is unaffected by removing the compactness constraints.

To remove the compactness constraints in the first line of Eq. (
159
),
it suffices to show that with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}^{c}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2},\qquad\inf_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}>C_{v}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}=0}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{2|1}^{*}+C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}.

(159)

Indeed, then

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\leq\max\Big\{\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),\;\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}^{c}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}\Big\}

(160)

\displaystyle\leq\max\Big\{\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathcal{I}_{2}}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),\;\sup_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}^{c}}\;\min_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}\Big\},

and we can bound the right-hand side using Eqs. (
158
) and (
159
).
We can combine Eqs. (
158
) and (
159
) to lower bound the min-max problem in the second line of Eq. (
145
) similarly.

We now show Eq. (
159
) using Lemma
E.6
.
First, consider
\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u})=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{2}}
.
By Lemma
D.2
and because
\mathbb{E}[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}\|_{2}]/\sqrt{n_{2}}\leq\zeta_{2}(\tau_{e_{2}}^{2}+\tau_{h_{2}}^{2})^{1/2}\leq C^{\prime}
(see Eq. (
64
) and Lemma
C.1
),
we have with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cp}

that
\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{u}(\boldsymbol{u}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}/\sqrt{n_{2}})\bigm|\boldsymbol{h}_{1}^{f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}\big]\leq C^{\prime}
.
Recalling the definition of
E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
(see Eq. (
144
)), we have for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
sufficiently small and
C_{u}
sufficiently large with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}
that
E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u|1}
.
The first bound in Eq. (
159
) now follows by a similar argument used to remove the compactness constraint in Eq. (
99
) in the proof of Lemma
E.6
.
For completeness, we repeat the details here.
Consider the event that
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
and
E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u|1}
.
Consider any
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}^{c}
.
Because the function
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
is concave, it is non-decreasing on the line segment joining
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}
to
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}
, and because
E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
is contained in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{u|1}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in E_{u|1}(\epsilon)
,
this line segment intersects with
E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\cap\mathcal{S}_{u|1}
at some point
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime\prime}
.
Then,

\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{u}\in E_{u|1}^{c}(\epsilon)\\
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u|1}\end{subarray}}\;\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}L_{2}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\leq\ell_{2|1}^{*}-C^{\prime}\epsilon^{2}.

(161)

Because this applies to all
\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\in S_{u}^{c}
, we conclude that with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon)e^{-\mathsf{c}(\epsilon)p}

the first bound in Eq. (
159
).
The second bound in Eq. (
159
) occurs by a similar argument, using
\phi_{v}(\boldsymbol{v})=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}
.

Provided we can prove Eqs. (
155
) and (
156
),
we have concluded Lemma
F.4
.
We establish these bounds in the next three sections.

F.3.1
The good conditional characterization event

To prove Eqs. (
155
) and (
156
),
we show that the upper and lower bounds on the min-max problem are implied by the occurrence of a certain high-probability event.
Recall the functions
\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{r})\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{r}
introduced in Section
E.3.1
.
The good conditional characterization event is

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta):=

(162)

\displaystyle\Big\{

\displaystyle\Big\|\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{h}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\,\tau_{h_{1}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{h}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}-(\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h}/\tau_{h_{1}})\boldsymbol{h}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\,\tau_{h_{2}}\rho_{h}^{\perp}}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{\mathsf{F}}<\epsilon,\;

\displaystyle\Big\|\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{cf},\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g}}{\sqrt{p}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}}{\sqrt{p}}\Big)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{f},\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{f},\frac{\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f}}{\sqrt{p}\,\tau_{g_{1}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{g}_{2}^{f}-(\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}/\tau_{g_{1}})\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f}}{\sqrt{p}\,\tau_{g_{2}}\rho_{g}^{\perp}}\Big)\Big]\Big\|_{\mathsf{F}}<\epsilon,\;

\displaystyle\big|\bar{\Omega}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{cf})-\omega_{k}\big|<\epsilon,\;\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}-\sqrt{n_{12}}\tau_{g_{1}}\zeta_{1}\Big|<\epsilon,\;\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}^{c}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}-\sqrt{n_{1}-n_{12}}\tau_{g_{1}}\zeta_{1}\Big|<\epsilon,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}-\tau_{e_{2}}\Big|<\epsilon,\;\frac{\|\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{v}_{1}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{g}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}<\epsilon,\;\frac{\|\mathsf{P}_{\boldsymbol{u}_{2}}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{h}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}<\epsilon,\;\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{cf}\in E_{v|1}(\Delta/2),\;\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{cf}\in E_{u|1}(\Delta/2)\;\Big\}.

Note the quantity
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{1}}
is at most of order 1, though it can be substantially smaller if
\mathcal{I}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{2}
is small.
In all other cases, we have normalized the arguments to
\boldsymbol{T}
so that their
\ell_{2}
norm is of order 1 (see Lemma
C.1
).
For future reference, we have written the expectations appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}
in Section
M.5
.
In words,
the good conditional characterization event is the event that several important quantities are close to their expectations.
It has high probability.

Lemma F.7

(Gordon’s good conditional characterization event)
.

Assume
A1
and
A2
.
There exist
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
and regression method-dependent
c^{\prime}>0
and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{c}:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
for
\epsilon,\Delta<c^{\prime}

with probability at least
1-\mathsf{C}(\epsilon,\Delta)e^{-p\mathsf{c}(\epsilon,\Delta)}
,
the event
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta)
occurs.

We prove Lemma
F.7
in Section
J.1
.

Remark F.8
.

We point out an asymmetry in the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta)
.
From the first regression, the random-design quantity
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}
is passed as an argument to
\boldsymbol{T}
, whereas from the second regression, the conditional fixed-design quantity
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{cf}
is passed as an argument to
\boldsymbol{T}
.
Note that in general,
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\neq\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{cf}
.
We point out this asymmetry to assure the reader that it is not a typo.
Our choice is driven by the requirements of analyzing the conditional auxilliary objective.
Note that the conditional auxilliary objective is defined using
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}
(via the definition of
\boldsymbol{h}_{cg}(\boldsymbol{v})
), not
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{cf}
.
On the other hand, we will approximate the minimizer of the conditional auxilliary objective by
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{cf}
.
The quantity
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
does not appear in the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta)
.
Indeed, it is not approximated well by quantities appearing in the conditional auxilliary objective.
See Sections
F.3.2
and
F.3.3
for complete details.

F.3.2
Lower bounds on the auxilliary min-max problem

In this section, we prove Eq. (
155
).
In particular, we seek a lower bound on

\min_{\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq C_{v}}\;\max_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{S}_{u}}\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}),

(163)

and also on the problem where we further restrict the minimization to
\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{v|1}^{c}(\epsilon)
.
It is enough to show that our lower bound holds on the event
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta)
for
\epsilon<c^{\prime}
,
\Delta=\Delta(\epsilon)<c^{\prime}
, and
c^{\prime}
taken sufficiently small and depending only on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{regr}}
, and the regression method.
We will henceforce assume we are on this event for
\epsilon,\Delta
for
c^{\prime}
sufficiently small, without repeatedly reminding the reader of this fact,
and all statements will be deterministic.
In particular,
as we derive more implications of
\mathcal{G}_{2|1}(\epsilon,\Delta)
,
we may need to take
c^{\prime}
smaller,
but will not track
c^{\prime}
or announce when an additional implication requires we shrink
c^{\prime}
further.
To make notation more compact,
we denote

\begin{gathered}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{N},2|1}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{u}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}},\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}}{p}}\boldsymbol{h}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f},\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{p}}\boldsymbol{h}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f},\frac{\boldsymbol{h}_{1,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\,\tau_{h_{1}}},\frac{\boldsymbol{h}_{2,\mathcal{I}_{2}}^{f}}{\sqrt{n_{2}}\,\tau_{h_{2}}}\Big)\Big],\\
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{P},2|1}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{T}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{f},\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{p}}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{f},\sqrt{\frac{n_{1}}{p}}\boldsymbol{g}_{1}^{f},\sqrt{\frac{n_{2}}{p}}\boldsymbol{g}_{2}^{f}\Big)\Big].\end{gathered}

(164)

We remind the reader that explicit expressions for these can be found in Section
M.5
.

The major steps in the analysis are similar to those in the proof of Eq. (
96
) from the marginal characterization.
We repeat those steps in the current context here:

1.

We replace
\ell_{2|1}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
by a function
